Consider the function

\Sigma_{m}(t):=|\{k\geq 1:\tfrac{m_{k}}{m_{k-1}}\leq t\}|.

Now (
7.8
) implies

(7.11)

2\Sigma_{m}(t)\leq\Sigma_{m}(Ct),\quad t>0.

It is well-known (cf.
[
58
]
and
[
47
]
) that
\omega_{m}
defined by

\omega_{m}(t)=\log(1/h_{m}(1/t))

satisfies
9
9

9

Note that the integral

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega_{M}(t)}{t^{2}}\,dt
converges if and only if
\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\Sigma_{M}(t)}{t^{2}}\,dt
converges, and that is the case if and only if
M
is
non-quasianalytic;
[
47
, Section 4]
.

(7.12)

\omega_{m}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\Sigma_{m}(u)}{u}\,du.

So (
7.11
) implies
2\omega_{m}(t)\leq\omega_{m}(Ct)
for all
t>0
, which is clearly equivalent to (
7.9
).

Let us check (
7.10
). Since
m
has moderate growth and is log-convex, there are constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\frac{m_{2k+1}}{m_{2k}}\leq C_{1}\frac{m_{2k}}{m_{2k-1}}\leq C_{2}\frac{m_{k}}{m_{k-1}}\leq C_{2}\frac{m_{k+1}}{m_{k}},\quad k\geq 1.

Fix
t>0
and let
\ell:=\Gamma_{m}(t)
. If
\ell=2k
,
then

\frac{1}{t}\leq\frac{m_{\ell+1}}{m_{\ell}}\implies\frac{1}{C_{2}t}\leq\frac{m_{k+1}}{m_{k}},

that is,
\Gamma_{m}(C_{2}t)\leq k=\tfrac{1}{2}\Gamma_{m}(t)
.
If
\ell=2k+1
, then we similarly see that

\Gamma_{m}(\frac{C_{2}}{C_{1}}t)\leq k=\tfrac{1}{2}(\Gamma_{m}(t)-1)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\Gamma_{m}(t)
.
Since
\Gamma_{m}
is decreasing, (
7.10
) follows.
∎

Moderate growth of the weight sequence is an essential technical tool in this section. So we make the following definition.

Definition 7.3
.

A weight sequence
M=(M_{k})
is called
strongly regular
if
M
is strongly non-quasianalytic, has moderate growth,
and
m
is log-convex.
Note that the last condition is actually for free:
a strongly non-quasianalytic weight sequence
M
is equivalent to a strongly log-convex weight sequence (that is still
strongly non-quasianalytic),
by
Corollary

4.8
.

7.3.
Optimal cutoff functions of Roumieu type

It turns out that there exist
optimal

\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}
cutoff functions, i.e.,
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying (
7.1
) and realizing (
7.2
) so that

\|\varphi\|^{M}_{\mathbb{R},\rho}\sim\frac{1}{h_{m}(\rho r\epsilon)},

if and only if
M
is strongly non-quasianalytic.
The existence of
\varphi
will follow from
Proposition

3.11
. The following lemma provides the necessary assumptions.
Recall that
\mu_{k}^{*}=\frac{\mu_{k}}{k}=\frac{m_{k}}{m_{k-1}}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
There is a constant
A\geq 1
such that for each integer
p\geq 1
there is a sequence
(\alpha^{p}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying

(7.13)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\alpha^{p}_{k}}{\alpha^{p}_{k+1}}\leq 1,\quad\alpha_{0}^{p}=1,\quad 0<\alpha^{p}_{k}\leq\frac{(\frac{A}{\mu_{p+1}^{*}})^{k}M_{k}}{h_{m}(\frac{1}{e\mu_{p}^{*}})}.

Proof.

We will show that, for a suitable constant
A\geq 1
, the family of sequences

\alpha^{p}_{k}:=\begin{cases}\big(\frac{A}{\mu_{p+1}^{*}}\big)^{k}M_{k}&\text{ if }k>p,\\
(2p)^{k}&\text{ if }k\leq p,\end{cases}

has the desired properties.
Since
M
has moderate growth,
\mu_{k+1}\lesssim M_{k}^{1/k}
and thus,
for some constant
C\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\alpha^{p}_{p}=2^{p}p^{p}\leq\Big(\frac{2C}{\mu_{p+1}^{*}}\Big)^{p}M_{p}.

So if
A
is chosen large enough, then

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\alpha_{k}^{p}}{\alpha^{p}_{k+1}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k<p}\frac{1}{2p}+\frac{\mu_{p+1}^{*}}{A}\sum_{k\geq p}\frac{1}{\mu_{k+1}}\leq 1,

since
M
is strongly non-quasianalytic.
The bound for
\alpha^{p}_{k}
(in (
7.13
)) is obvious for
k>p
,
since
h_{m}\leq 1
.
If
k\leq p
, then, for large enough
A
,

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha^{p}_{k}}{(\frac{A}{\mu_{p+1}^{*}})^{k}\,M_{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{k}p^{k}}{(\frac{A}{\mu_{p+1}^{*}})^{k}\,M_{k}}\leq\frac{\mu_{p+1}^{k}}{(\frac{A}{2})^{k}M_{k}}\leq\frac{\mu_{p}^{k}}{M_{k}}\leq\frac{\mu_{p}^{p}}{M_{p}}=\frac{\mu_{p}^{p}}{p!m_{p}}\leq\frac{(e\mu_{p}^{*})^{p}}{m_{p}}.

This implies the statement.
∎

Theorem 7.5

(
[
20
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

Let
M=(M_{k})
be a non-quasianalytic strongly log-convex weight sequence of moderate growth.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

M
is strongly non-quasianalytic.

(2)

There is a constant
C>0
such that for all
\rho,r,\epsilon>0
there exists
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying (
7.1
) and

(7.14)

\|\varphi\|^{M}_{\mathbb{R},\rho}\leq\frac{1}{h_{m}(C\rho r\epsilon)}.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)
We first consider the case
r=\epsilon=1
.
Let
A
be the constant from
Lemma

7.4
.
Fix
0<\eta\leq 2A
.
Since
\mu_{k}^{*}\nearrow\infty
,
there is an integer
p\geq 1
such that

(7.15)

\frac{2A}{\mu_{p+1}^{*}}<\eta\leq\frac{2A}{\mu_{p}^{*}}.

By
Lemma

7.4
, we may
apply
Proposition

3.11
(to
d_{k+1}=\frac{\alpha^{p}_{k}}{\alpha^{p}_{k+1}}
and
K=[-1,1]
) and
get
\varphi=\varphi_{\eta}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with

0\leq\varphi\leq 1
,
\varphi|_{[-1,1]}=1
,
\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subseteq[-2,2]
, and

\displaystyle|\varphi^{(k)}(t)|\leq 2^{k}\alpha_{k}^{p}\leq\frac{\eta^{k}M_{k}}{h_{m}(\frac{\eta}{6A})},\quad k\geq 1,

by (
7.13
) and (
7.15
).
For
\eta>2A
we put
\varphi_{\eta}:=\varphi_{2A}
; then since
h_{m}\leq 1
,

\displaystyle|\varphi^{(k)}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(2A)^{k}M_{k}}{h_{m}(\frac{2A}{6A})}\leq\frac{1}{h_{m}(\frac{1}{3})}\frac{\eta^{k}M_{k}}{h_{m}(\frac{\eta}{6A})}.

If
\delta:=1/h_{m}(\frac{1}{3})
then for every
\eta>0
,

|\varphi^{(k)}(t)|\leq\frac{(\delta\eta)^{k}M_{k}}{h_{m}(\frac{\eta}{6A})}.

This implies (2), for
r=\epsilon=1
.
For the general case it suffices to compose
\varphi_{\rho r\epsilon}

with an
odd smooth function
\theta:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\theta([-r,r])=[-1,1]
and

\theta(x)=\frac{x+r\epsilon-r}{r\epsilon}
for
x\geq r
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)
Let
\varphi
be as in (2) for given
r>0
and
\rho=\epsilon=1
.
Then the function
\psi(x):=\varphi(x-2r)\cdot h_{m}(Cr)
satisfies
\|\psi^{(k)}\|_{\mathbb{R}}\leq M_{k}
for all
k
and vanishes on
(-\infty,0]
.

Lemma

3.2
implies

h_{m}(Cr)=|\psi(r)|\leq\Big(\frac{2r}{\sum_{k\geq\ell}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}}\Big)^{\ell},\quad\ell\geq 1,

provided that
r<\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k\geq\ell}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}
.
For those
\ell
that satisfy
\frac{1}{C\mu_{\ell}^{*}}<\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k\geq\ell}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}
,
we obtain

h_{m}(\tfrac{1}{\mu^{*}_{\ell}})\leq\Big(\frac{\tfrac{2}{C}\tfrac{1}{\mu^{*}_{\ell}}}{\sum_{k\geq\ell}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}}\Big)^{\ell}.

On the other hand,

h_{m}(\tfrac{1}{\mu^{*}_{\ell}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{GaProp3}}}{{=}}m_{\ell-1}(\tfrac{1}{\mu^{*}_{\ell}})^{\ell-1}\geq H^{\ell-1}

for some constant
H>0
, since
m
has moderate growth. So we may conclude that
\sum_{k\geq\ell}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\mu^{*}_{\ell}}

for all
\ell
.
∎

7.4.
Optimal cutoff functions of Beurling type

There exist also optimal cutoff functions of Beurling type. They will play a crucial role in
Section

9
.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
Then there exist functions
(\varphi_{r})_{r>0}
in
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R})
with the following properties:

(1)

0\leq\varphi_{r}\leq 1
for all
r>0
.

(2)

\varphi_{r}|_{[-r,r]}=1
and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{r}\subseteq[-\frac{9}{8}r,\frac{9}{8}r]
for all
r>0
.

(3)

For each
\rho>0
there exist constants
A_{\rho}>0
and
b_{\rho}>0
, such that

(7.16)

\|\varphi_{r}\|^{M}_{\mathbb{R},\rho}\leq\frac{A_{\rho}}{h_{m}(b_{\rho}r)},\quad r>0.

The essential difference to
Theorem

7.5
is that the cutoff functions may be taken independent of
\rho
.
The dependence of the argument of
h_{m}
on
\rho
in (
7.14
) is however simpler than in (
7.16
);
the linear dependence in (
7.14
) will be used in the proof of
Theorem

8.4
.

Proof.

We may assume the setup of
Corollary

4.10
, in particular,
there is a constant
A>0
such that (
4.10
) holds.
Let
0<r\leq 8A
be fixed and take an integer
k=k(r)\geq 1
such that

\frac{8A}{\mu_{k}^{*}}<r\leq\frac{8A}{\mu_{k-1}^{*}}.

By
Proposition

3.11
(applied to
K=[-r,r]
and
\sum d_{j}
the left-hand side of (
4.10
)),
there exists
\varphi=\varphi_{r}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

with

0\leq\varphi\leq 1
,
\varphi|_{[-r,r]}=1
,
\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subseteq[-\frac{9}{8}r,\frac{9}{8}r]
, and

\displaystyle|\varphi_{r}^{(j)}|\leq\begin{cases}2^{j}\mu_{k}^{j}&\text{ if }0\leq j\leq k,\\
2^{j}\mu_{k}^{k}\frac{M_{j}}{M_{k}}\Big(\frac{k^{j-k}k!}{j!}\Big)^{\epsilon}&\text{ if }j>k.\end{cases}

Since
m
has moderate growth, we have
\mu^{*}_{k}\leq C\mu^{*}_{k-1}
for some
C\geq 1
. Thus

\frac{\mu_{k}^{k}}{M_{k}}=\frac{\mu_{k}^{k}}{k!\,m_{k}}\leq\frac{(e\mu_{k}^{*})^{k}}{m_{k}}\leq\frac{(Ce\mu_{k-1}^{*})^{k}}{m_{k}}\leq\Big(\frac{8ACe}{r}\Big)^{k}\frac{1}{m_{k}}.

Let
\rho\in(0,1)
be given.
If
j\leq k
, then
\mu_{k}^{j}\leq\mu_{k}^{k}\frac{M_{j}}{M_{k}}
, and hence

\displaystyle|\varphi_{r}^{(j)}|\leq 2^{j}M_{j}\Big(\frac{8ACe}{r}\Big)^{k}\frac{1}{m_{k}}\leq\rho^{j}M_{j}\Big(\frac{16ACe}{r\rho}\Big)^{k}\frac{1}{m_{k}}.

If
j>k
, then
\frac{k^{j-k}k!}{j!}\leq\frac{k^{j-k}}{(j-k)!}
, and so

\displaystyle|\varphi_{r}^{(j)}|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{j}M_{j}\Big(\frac{8ACe}{r}\Big)^{k}\frac{1}{m_{k}}\Big(\frac{k^{j-k}}{(j-k)!}\Big)^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\rho^{j}M_{j}\Big(\frac{16ACe}{r\rho}\Big)^{k}\frac{1}{m_{k}}\Big(\frac{(k(\frac{2}{\rho})^{1/\epsilon})^{j-k}}{(j-k)!}\Big)^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{j}M_{j}\Big(\frac{16ACe}{r\rho}\Big)^{k}\frac{1}{m_{k}}e^{\epsilon k(\frac{2}{\rho})^{1/\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=\rho^{j}M_{j}\frac{1}{(b_{\rho}r)^{k}m_{k}},\quad\text{ where }\quad b_{\rho}:=\frac{\rho}{16ACe^{1+\epsilon(\frac{2}{\rho})^{1/\epsilon}}}.

It follows that, for all
0<r\leq 8A
and all
\rho>0
,

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{r}\|^{M}_{\mathbb{R},\rho}\leq\frac{1}{h_{m}(b_{\rho}r)};

for
\rho\geq 1
the estimates are much simpler.
For
r>8A
we apply
Proposition

3.11
to
K=[-r,r]
and (
4.10
) with
k=1
.
This gives
\varphi_{r}
having the same bounds as
\varphi_{8A}
. Then

\|\varphi_{r}\|^{M}_{\mathbb{R},\rho}\leq\frac{1}{h_{m}(b_{\rho}8A)}\leq\frac{1}{h_{m}(b_{\rho}8A)}\frac{1}{h_{m}(b_{\rho}r)}=:\frac{A_{\rho}}{h_{m}(b_{\rho}r)},

since
h_{m}\leq 1
.
∎

7.5.
Whitney cubes

We denote by
d_{A}(z)=d(z,A)=\inf\{|z-y|:y\in A\}
the Euclidean distance of
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
to some set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proposition 7.7

(
[
87
, pp.167–170]
,
[
20
]
)
.

For each closed non-empty set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
there exists a countable collection of closed cubes
(Q_{j})_{j\geq 1}
with sides parallel to the coordinate
axes and the following properties.

(1)

\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A=\bigcup_{j\geq 1}Q_{j}
.

(2)

The interiors of the cubes
Q_{j}
are pairwise disjoint.

(3)

For each
j\geq 1
we have

\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}\leq\operatorname{dist}(Q_{j},A)\leq 4\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}.

(4)

For each
j\geq 1
let
Q_{j}^{*}
be image of
Q_{j}
under the dilation by the factor
\tfrac{9}{8}
with center the center of
Q_{j}
.
There exist constants
0<b_{0}\leq 1\leq B_{0}
such that for all
j\geq 1
and all
z\in Q_{j}^{*}
,

b_{0}\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}\leq d_{A}(z)\leq B_{0}\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}.

(5)

For each
j\geq 1
the number of
i\geq 1
with
Q_{i}^{*}\cap Q_{j}^{*}\neq\emptyset
is bounded by
12^{2n}
.

(6)

There exist constants
0<b_{1}\leq 1\leq B_{1}
such that for all
i,j\geq 0
with
Q_{i}^{*}\cap Q_{j}^{*}\neq\emptyset
we have

b_{1}\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}\leq\operatorname{diam}Q_{i}\leq B_{1}\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}.

The constants
b_{0},B_{0},b_{1},B_{1}
are independent of
A
.

A collection
(Q_{j})_{j\geq 1}
with these properties is called a
family of Whitney cubes for
A
.

7.6.
Optimal partitions of unity

Having optimal cutoff functions, it is a standard procedure to construct
optimal partitions of unity subordinate to a given family of Whitney cubes.
We begin with the Roumieu case.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a non-empty closed set and
(Q_{j})_{j\geq 1}
a family of Whitney cubes for
A
.
Then there exists

C_{1}\geq 1
such that for all
\epsilon>0
there is a family of
C^{\infty}
-functions

(\varphi_{j,\epsilon})_{j\geq 1}
satisfying

(1)

0\leq\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\leq 1
for all
j\geq 1
,

(2)

\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\subseteq Q^{*}_{j}
for all
j\geq 1
,

(3)

\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}(x)=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A
,

(4)

for all
j\geq 1
,
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A
,

|\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j,\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{\epsilon^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(C_{1}\epsilon\,d_{A}(x))}.

Proof.

By
Theorem

7.5
, there is a constant
C>1
such that for each
\rho>0
and each
r>0

there exist functions
\chi_{\rho,r}
such that

•

0\leq\chi_{\rho,r}(x)\leq 1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

•

\chi_{\rho,r}(x)=1
for all
x\in[-r,r]^{n}
,

•

\chi_{\rho,r}(x)=0
for all
x\not\in[-\frac{9}{8}r,\frac{9}{8}r]^{n}
,

•

for all
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

|\chi^{(\beta)}_{\rho,r}(x)|\leq\frac{\rho^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(C\rho r)}.

Let
x_{j}
be the center and
2s_{j}
the sidelength of
Q_{j}
. Set

\psi_{j,\epsilon}(x):=\chi_{\frac{\epsilon}{12^{2n}},s_{j}}(x-x_{j}).

Then
\psi_{j,\epsilon}
is
1
on
Q_{j}
and
0
outside
Q_{j}^{*}
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle|\psi^{(\beta)}_{j,\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{(\frac{\epsilon}{12^{2n}})^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(\frac{C\epsilon s_{j}}{12^{2n}})}\leq\frac{(\frac{\epsilon}{12^{2n}})^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(C_{0}\epsilon\,d_{A}(x))},\quad\text{ for }C_{0}:=\frac{C}{2\sqrt{n}12^{2n}B_{0}},

since
d_{A}(x)\leq B_{0}\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}=2\sqrt{n}B_{0}s_{j}
if
x\in Q^{*}_{j}
, by
Proposition

7.7
(4).
Now define
(\varphi_{j,\epsilon})_{j\geq 1}
by

(7.17)

\varphi_{1,\epsilon}:=\psi_{1,\epsilon},\quad\varphi_{j,\epsilon}:=\psi_{j,\epsilon}\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}(1-\psi_{k,\epsilon}),~j\geq 2.

It is clear that
0\leq\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\leq 1
and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\subseteq Q^{*}_{j}
for all
j\geq 1
.
To see (3) observe that, for all
j\geq 1
,

\varphi_{1,\epsilon}+\cdots+\varphi_{j,\epsilon}=1-(1-\psi_{1,\epsilon})\cdots(1-\psi_{j,\epsilon}).

If
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A
, then
x\in Q_{i_{x}}
for some
i_{x}\geq 1
and thus
\psi_{i_{x},\epsilon}(x)=1
.
Since the family
(\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j,\epsilon})_{j\geq 1}
is locally finite, it follows that
\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}(x)=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A
.
Finally, let us check (4).
In the product defining
\varphi_{j,\epsilon}
at most
12^{2n}
factors are different from
1
, by
Proposition

7.7
(5).
Thus

|\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j,\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{\epsilon^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(C_{0}\epsilon\,d_{A}(x))^{12^{2n}}}.

By
Lemma

7.2
, there is a constant
D>1
such that
h_{m}(t)\leq h_{m}(Dt)^{12^{2n}}
for all
t>0
.
Thus, we obtain (4) with
C_{1}=C_{0}/D
.
∎

Let us come to the Beurling case.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a non-empty closed set and
(Q_{j})_{j\geq 1}
a family of Whitney cubes for
A
.
Then there is a family of
C^{\infty}
-functions

(\varphi_{j})_{j\geq 1}
satisfying

(1)

0\leq\varphi_{j}\leq 1
for all
j\geq 1
,

(2)

\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j}\subseteq Q^{*}_{j}
for all
j\geq 1
,

(3)

\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j}(x)=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A
,

(4)

for each
\rho>0
there exist
C_{1},\tau_{1}>0
such that
for all
j\geq 1
,
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A
,

|\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j}(x)|\leq C_{1}\frac{\rho^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(\tau_{1}\,d_{A}(x))}.

Proof.

By
Theorem

7.6
, there exist smooth functions
(\chi_{r})_{r>0}
such that

•

0\leq\chi_{r}(x)\leq 1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

•

\chi_{r}(x)=1
for all
x\in[-r,r]^{n}
,

•

\chi_{r}(x)=0
for all
x\not\in[-\frac{9}{8}r,\frac{9}{8}r]^{n}
,

•

for each
\rho>0
there exist
A_{\rho}\geq 1
and
b_{\rho}>0
such that for all
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

|\chi^{(\beta)}_{r}(x)|\leq A_{\rho}\frac{\rho^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(b_{\rho}r)}.

If
x_{j}
denotes the center of the cube
Q_{j}
and
2s_{j}
its sidelength, we set

\psi_{j}(x):=\chi_{s_{j}}(x-x_{j}).

Then

\psi_{j}|_{Q_{j}}=1
,
\operatorname{supp}\psi_{j}\subseteq Q_{j}^{*}
, and

\displaystyle|\psi^{(\beta)}_{j}(x)|\leq A_{\rho}\frac{\rho^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(b_{\rho}s_{j})}\leq A_{\rho}\frac{\rho^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(b_{\rho}^{\prime}d_{A}(x))}.

In analogy to (
7.17
) we define the family
(\varphi_{j})_{j\geq 1}
which clearly has the properties (1)–(3).
The fact that at most
12^{2n}
factors in the product defining
\varphi_{j}
are different from
1
together with the estimates for
\psi_{j}

and (
7.9
)
easily imply (4).
∎

8.
Extension of Whitney ultrajets

If we restrict an
\mathcal{E}^{[M]}
-function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then Taylor’s theorem
implies necessary conditions which lead to the notion of Whitney ultrajets.

8.1.
Whitney ultrajets

Let
M=(M_{k})
be a weight sequence.
Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a compact set.
A
Whitney ultrajet of class
\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}
on
K
is a Whitney jet

F=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{E}(K)
such that
there exist
C>0
and
\rho\geq 1

with

(8.1)

\displaystyle|F^{\alpha}(x)|\leq C\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|},\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},~x\in K,

(8.2)

\displaystyle|(R^{p}_{x}F)^{\alpha}(y)|\leq C\rho^{p+1}\,|\alpha|!\,m_{p+1}\,|x-y|^{p+1-|\alpha|},\quad p\in\mathbb{N},\,|\alpha|\leq p,~x,y\in K.

A
Whitney ultrajet of class
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}
on
K
is a Whitney jet

F=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{E}(K)
with the property that for each
\rho>0
there exists
C>0
such that
(
8.1
) and (
8.2
) hold.

The set of Whitney ultrajets of class
\mathcal{E}^{[M]}
on
K
is denoted by
\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(K)
.
For a closed set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
let

\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(A):=\{F\in\mathcal{E}(A):F|_{K}\in\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(K)\text{ for all compact }K\subseteq A\}

be the set of
Whitney ultrajets of class
\mathcal{E}^{[M]}
on
A
.

We endow
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)
with the projective limit topology with respect to the system of seminorms

p_{K,1/\ell}(F):=\|F\|^{M}_{K,1/\ell}+|F|^{M}_{K,1/\ell}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
and
K\subseteq A
compact,
where

(8.3)

\|F\|^{M}_{K,\rho}:=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{\|F^{\alpha}\|_{K}}{\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}}

and
10
10

10

Note that
|\alpha|!\,m_{p+1}\leq\frac{M_{p+1}}{(p+1-|\alpha|)!}\leq 2^{p+1}|\alpha|!\,m_{p+1}
.

(8.4)

|F|^{M}_{K,\rho}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in K\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\sup_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{|\alpha|\leq p}|(R^{p}_{x}F)^{\alpha}(y)|\frac{(p+1-|\alpha|)!}{|x-y|^{p+1-|\alpha|}\rho^{p+1}M_{p+1}}.

Similarly, the Roumieu space

\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(A)=\{F\in\mathcal{E}(A):\;\forall K\subseteq_{cp}A\;\exists m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}:p_{K,m}(F)<\infty\}

is endowed with its natural locally convex topology.
Sometimes we shall need the Banach space
\mathcal{E}^{M}_{\rho}(K):=\{F\in\mathcal{E}(K):p_{K,\rho}(F)<\infty\}
.

Remark 8.1
.

Let
k
be a positive integer.
A compact subset
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is called
Whitney
k
-regular
if there is a constant
C>0

such that for all
x,y\in K
there exists a rectifiable path
\gamma
in
K
joining
x
and
y
such that

\operatorname{length}(\gamma)\leq C|x-y|^{1/k}.

It is not difficult to see that (
8.1
) implies (
8.2
) provided that
K
is Whitney
1
-regular, e.g.,
if
K
is convex.
This is no longer true if
K
is only Whitney
k
-regular for some
k>1
; cf.
[
71
, 1.9]
.

The goal of this section is to prove that Whitney ultrajets admit extensions preserving the class if the weight sequence
M

is strongly regular. The proof of the Roumieu case is based on the existence of optimal cutoff functions and the extension problem for
the singleton (the Borel map). The Beurling case can be extracted from the Roumieu case by a reduction argument based on

Lemma

3.4
.

In
Section

9
we will prove that extension operators always exist in the Beurling case.
As a by-product it gives an alternative direct proof that extensions exist.

8.2.
Extension of Whitney ultrajets of Roumieu type

Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a non-empty closed set and
F\in\mathcal{E}(A)
a Whitney jet on
A
.
A function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is said to be a
local extension of
F
in x
if

j^{\infty}_{\{x\}}f=F(x).

The next lemma provides local extensions in a uniform way.

Lemma 8.2

(
[
20
]
,
[
15
, Lemma 3.8]
)
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a compact non-empty set and
F\in\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(K)
.
Then there exists
\rho>0
such that
for all
x\in K
there exists
f_{x}\in\mathcal{B}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
j^{\infty}_{\{x\}}f_{x}=F(x)
and

(8.5)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in K}\|f_{x}\|^{M}_{\mathbb{R}^{n},\rho}<\infty.

Proof.

By assumption,
\{F(x):x\in K\}
is a bounded subset of
\Lambda^{\{M\}}
.
We know that
j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}:\mathcal{B}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\Lambda^{\{M\}}
is surjective, by
Theorem

4.4
.
Since both spaces are Silva spaces, each bounded set in
\Lambda^{\{M\}}
is the image of a bounded set in
\mathcal{B}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Thus, there is a bounded set
B
in
\mathcal{B}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that for all
x\in K

there exists
f_{x}\in B
with
j^{\infty}_{\{x\}}f_{x}=F(x)
.
This also implies (
8.5
).
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

In the setting of the previous lemma,
there exist
D,\sigma>0

such that for all

x,y\in K
,
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
we have

(8.6)

|(f_{x}-f_{y})^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq D\sigma^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\sigma(|z-x|+|z-y|)).

Proof.

Let
x,y\in K
be fixed. Take
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
|\alpha|\leq p
.
We have

\displaystyle T_{x}^{p}F(z)-T_{y}^{p}F(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\leq p}\frac{(z-x)^{\beta}}{\beta!}(T_{x}^{p}F-T_{y}^{p}F)^{(\beta)}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\leq p}\frac{(z-x)^{\beta}}{\beta!}(R_{y}^{p}F-R_{x}^{p}F)^{\beta}(x)=\sum_{\beta\leq p}\frac{(z-x)^{\beta}}{\beta!}(R^{p}_{y}F)^{\beta}(x).

Using (
8.2
), we find that there exist
A,\tau>0
such that

\displaystyle|(T_{x}^{p}F-T_{y}^{p}F)^{(\alpha)}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq A\tau^{p+1}M_{p+1}\frac{(|z-x|+|x-y|)^{p+1-|\alpha|}}{(p+1-|\alpha|)!}

\displaystyle\leq A(2\tau)^{p+1}M_{p+1}\frac{(|z-x|+|z-y|)^{p+1-|\alpha|}}{(p+1-|\alpha|)!}.

By Taylor’s formula and
j^{\infty}_{\{x\}}f_{x}=F(x)
,

\displaystyle f_{x}(z)-T_{x}^{p}F(z)=\sum_{|\beta|=p+1}\frac{(z-x)^{\beta}}{\beta!}f_{x}^{(\beta)}(x+t(z-x)),\quad t\in[0,1],

and thus

(8.7)

\displaystyle|(f_{x}-T_{x}^{p}F)^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq C\rho^{p+1}M_{p+1}\frac{|z-x|^{p+1-|\alpha|}}{(p+1-|\alpha|)!},\quad|\alpha|\leq p.

It follows that, for suitable constants
D,\sigma_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle|(f_{x}-f_{y})^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq D\sigma_{1}^{p+1}M_{p+1}\frac{(|z-x|+|z-y|)^{p+1-|\alpha|}}{(p+1-|\alpha|)!},

for all
x,y\in K
,
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
, and

|\alpha|\leq p
. Using that
M
has moderate growth, we infer

\displaystyle|(f_{x}-f_{y})^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq D\sigma^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}\cdot(\sigma(|z-x|+|z-y|))^{p+1-|\alpha|}m_{p+1-|\alpha|}

for all
p\geq|\alpha|
. This implies (
8.6
).
∎

Theorem 8.4
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
For each non-empty compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
the mapping
j^{\infty}_{K}:\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(K)
is surjective.

Proof.

Let
F\in\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(K)
satisfy (
8.1
) and (
8.2
).
By
Lemma

8.2
, there are
f_{x}\in\mathcal{B}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
x\in K
, satisfying
j^{\infty}_{\{x\}}f_{x}=F(x)
for all
x\in K

and (
8.5
).
Let
(Q_{j})_{j\geq 1}
be a family of Whitney cubes for
K
.
Let
\epsilon>0
(to be specified later).
By
Proposition

7.8
, there is
a family of
C^{\infty}
-functions
(\varphi_{j,\epsilon})_{j\geq 1}
satisfying

0\leq\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\leq 1
,
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\subseteq Q_{j}^{*}
,
\sum_{j}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}=1
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K
,
and

(8.8)

|\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j,\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{\epsilon^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(C_{1}\epsilon\,d_{K}(x))},\quad\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n},~x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K,

where
C_{1}>0
is independent of
\epsilon
.
Let
x_{j}
denote the center of
Q_{j}
and let

\hat{x}_{j}\in K
be such that
|x_{j}-\hat{x}_{j}|=d_{K}(x_{j})
. We define

(8.9)

f(z):=\begin{cases}\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}(z)\,f_{\hat{x}_{j}}(z),&\text{ if }z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K,\\
F^{0}(z),&\text{ if }z\in K.\end{cases}

Clearly,
f
is
C^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K
.

Claim
.

There exist constants
D_{1},\sigma_{1}>0
such that, for
x\in K
,

(8.10)

\displaystyle|(f-f_{x})^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq D_{1}\sigma_{1}^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\sigma_{1}|z-x|),\quad z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K.

We must estimate

(8.11)

\displaystyle(f-f_{x})^{(\alpha)}(z)=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j,\epsilon}(z)\,(f_{\hat{x}_{j}}-f_{x})^{(\alpha-\beta)}(z).

Let us first treat the term with
\beta=0
.
If
z\in\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\subseteq Q_{j}^{*}
, then

(8.12)

\displaystyle|z-\hat{x}_{j}|\leq|z-x_{j}|+|x_{j}-\hat{x}_{j}|\leq\frac{9}{8}\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}+d_{K}(x_{j})\lesssim d_{K}(z)\leq|z-x|,

by
Proposition

7.7
(3)–(4).
Thus, by
Lemma

8.3
, there exist
D,\sigma>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}(z)\,\big|(f_{\hat{x}_{j}}-f_{x})^{(\alpha)}(z)\big|\leq D\sigma^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\sigma|z-x|).

Now we consider the terms with
\beta\neq 0
in (
8.11
). To this end let
\hat{z}
be a point in
K
with
|\hat{z}-z|=d_{K}(z)
.
Since
\beta\neq 0
,
\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{(\beta)}(z)=0
and thus

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j,\epsilon}(z)\,(f_{\hat{x}_{j}}-f_{x})^{(\alpha-\beta)}(z)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j,\epsilon}(z)\,(f_{\hat{x}_{j}}-f_{\hat{z}})^{(\alpha-\beta)}(z).

We infer from
Lemma

8.3
, (
8.8
), (
8.12
), and
Proposition

7.7
(5) that there exist constants
D,\sigma>0
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 1}|\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j,\epsilon}(z)||(f_{\hat{x}_{j}}-f_{\hat{z}})^{(\alpha-\beta)}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq 12^{2n}D\epsilon^{|\beta|}\sigma^{|\alpha-\beta|}M_{|\beta|}M_{|\alpha-\beta|}\frac{h_{m}(\sigma d_{K}(z))}{h_{m}(C_{1}\epsilon\,d_{K}(z))}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{hmg}}}{{\leq}}12^{2n}D\epsilon^{|\beta|}\sigma^{|\alpha-\beta|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(C\sigma d_{K}(z))

if we choose
\epsilon:=C\sigma/C_{1}
, where
C
is the constant from (
7.9
). The claim follows.

In order to show that
f
is a
C^{\infty}
-function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
j^{\infty}_{K}f=F
, let, for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

f^{\alpha}(z):=\begin{cases}f^{(\alpha)}(z)&\text{ if }z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K,\\
F^{\alpha}(z)&\text{ if }z\in K.\end{cases}

We prove that
f=f^{0}
is a
C^{\infty}
-function with
f^{(\alpha)}=f^{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
.
First we show that all
f^{\alpha}
are continuous.
This is clear near points
x\not\in K
. So let
x\in K
.
Now if
z\in K
, then

|f^{\alpha}(z)-f^{\alpha}(x)|=|F^{\alpha}(z)-F^{\alpha}(x)|=|(R^{|\alpha|}_{x}F)^{\alpha}(z)|\to 0\quad\text{ as }z\to x,

by (
8.2
).
If
z\not\in K
, then

\displaystyle|f^{\alpha}(z)-f^{\alpha}(x)|

\displaystyle=|f^{(\alpha)}(z)-f_{x}^{(\alpha)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|f^{(\alpha)}(z)-f_{x}^{(\alpha)}(z)|+|f_{x}^{(\alpha)}(z)-f_{x}^{(\alpha)}(x)|\to 0\quad\text{ as }z\to x,

by (
8.10
) and since
f_{x}^{(\alpha)}
is continuous.
At this point we may refer to the lemma of Hestenes
[
43
, Lemma 1]
or argue as follows:
If
\delta_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
denotes the multiindex with
(\delta_{i})_{j}=\delta_{ij}
(the Kronecker symbol), then for
x\in K
and
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle|f^{\alpha}(z)-f^{\alpha}(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{n}(z_{i}-x_{i})f^{\alpha+\delta_{i}}(x)|=|f^{\alpha}(z)-(T_{x}^{|\alpha|+1}F)^{(\alpha)}(z)|=o(|z-x|),

by (
8.2
), (
8.7
), and (
8.10
). It means that
f^{\alpha}
is
C^{1}
and
\partial_{x_{i}}f^{\alpha}=f^{\alpha+\delta_{i}}
.
The assertion follows.

Finally, we claim that
f\in\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By (
8.1
), it suffices to consider
z\not\in K
.
Then the claim follows from

\displaystyle|f^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq|f_{\hat{z}}^{(\alpha)}(z)|+|(f-f_{\hat{z}})^{(\alpha)}(z)|,

(
8.5
), and (
8.10
).
∎

8.3.
Extension of Whitney ultrajets of Beurling type

The Beurling case may be reduced to the Roumieu case by means of the next lemma.

Lemma 8.5

(
[
27
, Proposition 17]
)
.

Let
M=(M_{k})
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
For any non-negative sequence
L=(L_{k})
with
L\lhd M

there exists a strongly regular weight sequence
N=(N_{k})
with
L\preccurlyeq N\lhd M
.

Proof.

There is a decreasing sequence
(\epsilon_{k})_{k\geq 1}
tending to
0
with
L_{k}\leq\epsilon_{1}\cdots\epsilon_{k}M_{k}

for all
k\geq 1
; it suffices to take
\epsilon_{k}:=\sup_{j\geq k}(\frac{L_{k}}{M_{k}})^{1/k}
.
Since
M
is strongly non-quasianalytic, there is a constant
C>0
such that

(8.13)

\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}\leq C\frac{k}{\mu_{k}},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Applying
Lemma

3.4
to
\alpha_{k}=\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}
,
\beta_{k}=\max\{\epsilon_{k},\frac{k}{\mu_{k}}\}
, and
\gamma_{k}=\frac{k}{\mu_{k}}
,
we find a sequence
(\theta_{k})_{k\geq 1}
with
\theta_{k}\nearrow\infty

such that

(8.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{\theta_{j}}{\mu_{j}}\leq 8\theta_{k}\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{1}{\mu_{j}},\quad\text{ for all }k\geq 1,

\theta_{k}\epsilon_{k}\to 0
, and
\frac{k\theta_{k}}{\mu_{k}}\searrow 0
. Define

\nu_{k}:=\frac{\mu_{k}}{\theta_{k}}
,
k\geq 1
, and
\nu_{0}:=0
.
Then
N_{k}:=\nu_{0}\nu_{1}\cdots\nu_{k}
is a strongly log-convex weight sequence
that is strongly non-quasianalytic, by (
8.13
) and (
8.14
),
and has moderate growth,

\frac{N_{j+k}}{N_{j}N_{k}}=\frac{M_{j+k}}{M_{j}M_{k}}\frac{\theta_{1}\cdots\theta_{j}\theta_{1}\cdots\theta_{k}}{\theta_{1}\cdots\theta_{j+k}}\leq\frac{M_{j+k}}{M_{j}M_{k}}.

To see
L\preccurlyeq N\lhd M
observe that

(\frac{L_{k}}{N_{k}})^{1/k}=(\frac{L_{k}}{M_{k}})^{1/k}(\theta_{1}\cdots\theta_{k})^{1/k}\leq(\theta_{1}\epsilon_{1}\cdots\theta_{k}\epsilon_{k})^{1/k}

is bounded and
(\frac{M_{k}}{N_{k}})^{1/k}=(\theta_{1}\cdots\theta_{k})^{1/k}\to\infty
.
∎

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
For each non-empty compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
the mapping
j^{\infty}_{K}:\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(K)
is surjective.

Proof.

Let
F=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(K)
. Set
L_{0}:=\|F^{0}\|_{K}
and

L_{k}:=\max\Big\{\sup_{|\alpha|=k}\|F^{\alpha}\|_{K},\sup_{x\neq y\in K,\,|\alpha|\leq k-1}\frac{k!\,|(R^{k-1}_{x}F)^{\alpha}(y)|}{|\alpha|!\,|y-x|^{k-|\alpha|}}\Big\},\quad k\geq 1.

Then
L\lhd M
and, by
Lemma

8.5
, there is a strongly regular weight sequence

N
with
L\preccurlyeq N\lhd M
. So
F\in\mathcal{E}^{\{N\}}(K)
and it has an extension
f\in\mathcal{E}^{\{N\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
by
Theorem

8.4
, which is also an element of
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
∎

8.4.
Extension from closed sets

It is now easy to get extension theorems from arbitrary closed subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
For each non-empty closed set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
the mapping
j^{\infty}_{A}:\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(A)
is surjective.

Proof.

This follows easily from
Theorems

8.4
and
8.6
, since
\mathcal{E}^{[M]}
admits partitions of unity. Indeed,
fix
F\in\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(A)
.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
set
U_{k}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:k-2<|x|<k\}
; note that
U_{k}
,
k\geq 2
, are open shells and

U_{1}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|x|<1\}
.
There exist functions
\varphi_{k}\in\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
0\leq\varphi_{k}\leq 1
,
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{k}\subseteq U_{k}
, and

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\varphi_{k}=1
.
For each
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
the jet
F_{k}:=F|_{\overline{U}_{k}}
belongs to

\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(A\cap\overline{U}_{k})
and thus has an extension

f_{k}\in\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, i.e.,

j^{\infty}_{A\cap\overline{U}_{k}}(f_{k})=F_{k}
.
Then

f:=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\varphi_{k}f_{k}

is a function in
\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
since on any compact set the sum is finite.
Each
x\in A
belongs to at most two consecutive sets
U_{\ell}
,
U_{\ell+1}
.
Thus, for each
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

\displaystyle f^{(\alpha)}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\varphi^{(\beta)}_{\ell}(x)f^{(\alpha-\beta)}_{\ell}(x)+\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\varphi^{(\beta)}_{\ell+1}(x)f^{(\alpha-\beta)}_{\ell+1}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\partial^{\beta}(\varphi_{\ell}(x)+\varphi_{\ell+1}(x))F^{\alpha-\beta}(x)=F^{\alpha}(x),

since all summands with
|\beta|>0
vanish.
∎

8.5.
Analytic extensions

The ultradifferentiable extension in
Theorem

8.7
can be made real analytic
on the complement of
A
. This follows from a result of Schmets and Validivia
[
83
]
.
A different proof due to Langenbruch
[
56
, Theorem 13]
is based on a general
approximation theorem of Whitney type (cf.
[
92
, Lemma 6]
) which is interesting in its own right.
In fact it is a special case of a quite general approximation theorem based
on a surjectivity criterion for continuous linear maps between Fréchet spaces.

Theorem 8.8

(
[
56
, Theorem 7]
)
.

Let
M=(M_{k})
be a non-quasianalytic weight sequence.
Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open and
\eta:\Omega\to(0,\infty)
continuous.
For any
f\in\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\Omega)
there exists
g\in\mathcal{H}(\Omega^{*})
such that

|f^{(\alpha)}(x)-g^{(\alpha)}(x)|\leq\eta(x)^{|\alpha|+1}M_{|\alpha|}\quad\text{ for all }x\in\Omega,~\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}.

Here
\Omega^{*}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:\mathrm{Re}(z)\in\Omega,\,|\mathrm{Im}(z)|<\operatorname{dist}(\mathrm{Re}(z),\partial\Omega)\}
.
We may assume that
g|_{\Omega}
is real-valued.

Theorem 8.9

(
[
56
, Theorem 13]
)
.

Let
M=(M_{k})
be a non-quasianalytic weight sequence and
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
open.
There exists a continuous linear mapping
T:\mathcal{B}^{[M]}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{B}^{[M]}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

T(f)
is an analytic
\Omega
-modification of
f
, i.e.,

T(f)|_{\Omega}
is real analytic and
j^{\infty}_{A}T(f)=j^{\infty}_{A}f
, where
A:=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Omega
.

9.
Continuous linear extension operators

In this section we address the question whether there exist continuous linear extension operators.
We concentrate on the Beurling case for which the question has an affirmative answer.
In
Section

9.4
we comment briefly on the Roumieu case which has a negative answer most of the time.

Let
M=(M_{k})
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a non-empty closed set.
By
Theorem

8.7
, the following sequence is exact:

(9.1)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ker j^{\infty}_{A}~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\infty}_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

We will show that the sequence (
9.1
) splits: there exists
a continuous linear right-inverse
E_{A}:\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)\to\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of
j^{\infty}_{A}
,
that is
j^{\infty}_{A}\circ E_{A}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)}
. We say that
E_{A}
is an
extension operator
.

We give an elementary constructive proof based on the fact that the Borel map is split surjective
and on the existence of optimal cutoff functions.
In
Section

9.3
we shall briefly comment on the alternative approach based on
the splitting theorem of Vogt and Wagner
[
91
]
.

9.1.
The existence of extension operators is a local property

We need a variant of
Lemma

8.3
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
For each
\rho>0
there exist
C,\sigma>0
such that the following holds.
If
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is compact and
H\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is compact convex with
K\subseteq\operatorname{int}H
,

x_{1},x_{2}\in K
, and

f_{1},f_{2}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
F\in\mathcal{E}(K)
satisfy
j^{\infty}_{\{x_{1}\}}f_{1}=F(x_{1})
,
j^{\infty}_{\{x_{2}\}}f_{2}=F(x_{2})
,
and

\|f_{1}\|^{M}_{H,\sigma}<\infty,\quad\|f_{2}\|^{M}_{H,\sigma}<\infty,\quad|F|^{M}_{K,\sigma}<\infty,

then for all
z\in H\setminus K
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

|(f_{1}-f_{2})^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq(\|f_{1}\|^{M}_{H,\sigma}+\|f_{2}\|^{M}_{H,\sigma}+|F|^{M}_{K,\sigma})\cdot C\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\rho(|z-x_{1}|+|z-x_{2}|)).

Proof.

As in the proof of
Lemma

8.3
we find, for
|\alpha|\leq p
,

|(T^{p}_{x_{1}}F-T^{p}_{x_{2}}F)^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq|F|^{M}_{K,\sigma}(2\sigma)^{p+1}M_{p+1}\frac{(|z-x_{1}|+|z-x_{2}|)^{p+1-|\alpha|}}{(p+1-|\alpha|)!}.

Since
j^{\infty}_{\{x_{i}\}}f_{i}=F(x_{i})
,

\displaystyle|(f_{i}-T^{p}_{x_{i}}F)^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq\|f_{i}\|^{M}_{H,\sigma}\sigma^{p+1}M_{p+1}\frac{(|z-x_{i}|)^{p+1-|\alpha|}}{(p+1-|\alpha|)!},\quad i=1,2,

as in (
8.7
).
Since
M
has moderate growth,
M_{p+1}\leq D^{p+1}M_{|\alpha|}M_{p+1-|\alpha|}
for some
D>0
. Then the triangle inequality gives
for all
p\geq|\alpha|
,

\displaystyle|(f_{1}-f_{2})^{(\alpha)}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq(2\sigma D)^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}m_{p+1-|\alpha|}(2\sigma D(|z-x_{1}|+|z-x_{2}|))^{p+1-|\alpha|}

\displaystyle\qquad\times(\|f_{1}\|^{M}_{H,\sigma}+\|f_{2}\|^{M}_{H,\sigma}+|F|^{M}_{K,\sigma})

and the assertion follows easily.
∎

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a closed non-empty set.
A continuous linear mapping
E:\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)\to\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is said to be a
local extension operator for
A
in
x

if

j^{\infty}_{\{x\}}E(F)=F(x),\quad\text{ for all }F\in\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A).

Clearly,
E
is an extension operator for
A
if it is a local extension operator for
A
in
every

x\in A
.

Proposition 9.2

(
[
37
, Lemma 3.6]
)
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a closed non-empty set.
Suppose that for each
x\in\partial A
there exists a local extension operator
E_{x}
for
A
in
x

and that
\{E_{x}:x\in\partial A\}
is locally equicontinuous in
L(\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A),\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.
Then there exists an extension operator
E:\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)\to\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

Let
(Q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of Whitney cubes for
A
(cf.
Proposition

7.7
) and let
(\varphi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
be the
partition of unity provided by
Proposition

7.9
.
For each
j
choose a point
x_{j}\in\partial A
such that
d_{Q_{j}}(x_{j})=\operatorname{dist}(A,Q_{j})
.
For
F\in\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)
define

E(F)(z):=\begin{cases}\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j}(z)E_{x_{j}}(F)(z)&\text{ if }z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A,\\
F^{0}(z)&\text{ if }z\in A.\end{cases}

Clearly,
E(F)
is of class
C^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus A
.
Recall that
p_{K,\sigma}(F):=\|F\|^{M}_{K,\sigma}+|F|^{M}_{K,\sigma}<\infty
for all compact
K\subseteq A
and
\sigma>0
.

Claim
.

For each compact convex
H\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}

and for each
\rho>0

there exist
C,\sigma,\tau>0
and a compact subset
K\subseteq A

such that
for all
x\in\partial A\cap H
,
z\in H\setminus A
with
|x-z|\leq\frac{b_{0}}{7}
(with
b_{0}
from
Proposition

7.7
),

F\in\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)
, and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

(9.2)

|(E(F)-E_{x}(F))^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq C\,p_{K,\sigma}(F)\,\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\tau|x-z|).

Let us prove the claim.
By
Lemma

7.2
, there is
C>1
such that

(9.3)

h_{m}(t)\leq h_{m}(Ct)^{2},\quad t>0.

Let a compact convex set
H\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\rho>0
be fixed.
By
Proposition

7.9
, there exist positive
C_{1}=C_{1}(\rho)
and
\tau_{1}=\tau_{1}(\rho)
such that, for all
j
,

(9.4)

|\varphi_{j}^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq C_{1}\frac{\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}}{h_{m}(\tau_{1}d_{A}(z))},\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\,z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Set

(9.5)

\rho_{2}:=\min\{\rho,\tfrac{b_{0}\tau_{1}}{7C}\}.

We invoke
Lemma

9.1
for
\rho_{2}
: there exist

C_{2},\sigma_{2}>0
such that the assertion of the lemma
holds for
\rho_{2}
,
C_{2}
,
\sigma_{2}
(instead of
\rho
,
C
,
\sigma
in the lemma).

For
\epsilon>0
let
H_{\epsilon}:=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:d_{H}(z)\leq\epsilon\}
be the closed
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
H
.
By assumption, the set
\{E_{x}:x\in\partial A\cap H_{2}\}
is equicontinuous in
L(\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A),\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.
It follows that there exist constants
\sigma_{3}\geq\sigma_{2}
,
C_{3}\geq 1
and a compact set
K\subseteq A
with
A\cap H_{2}\subseteq K

such that

\|E_{x}(F)\|^{M}_{H_{2},\sigma_{2}}\leq C_{3}\,p_{K,\sigma_{3}}(F),\quad F\in\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A),~x\in\partial A\cap H_{2}.

Then, by the assertion of
Lemma

9.1
, for all
x,y\in\partial A\cap H_{1}
,
z\in H_{1}\setminus A
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

(9.6)

\displaystyle|(E_{x}(F)-E_{y}(F))^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq C_{4}\,p_{K,\sigma_{3}}(F)\,\rho_{2}^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\rho_{2}(|z-x|+|z-y|)),

for some
C_{4}\geq 1
.

We are ready to estimate

(E(F)-E_{x}(F))^{(\alpha)}(z)=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j}^{(\beta)}(z)(E_{x_{j}}(F)-E_{x}(F))^{(\alpha-\beta)}(z),

where
x\in\partial A\cap H
and
z\in H\setminus A
with
|x-z|\leq\frac{b_{0}}{7}
.
By
Proposition

7.7
, for each
j\geq 1
and
z\in Q_{j}^{*}
,

\displaystyle|x_{j}-z|

\displaystyle\leq d_{Q_{j}}(x_{j})+\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}^{*}=\operatorname{dist}(A,Q_{j})+\tfrac{9}{8}\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}\leq 6\operatorname{diam}Q_{j}\leq\tfrac{6}{b_{0}}d_{A}(z).

Consequently (since
b_{0}\leq 1
),

|x-x_{j}|\leq|x-z|+|z-x_{j}|\leq|x-z|+\tfrac{6}{b_{0}}d_{A}(z)\leq\tfrac{7}{b_{0}}|x-z|\leq 1

and thus
x_{j}\in H_{1}
.
So, by (
9.6
), for the term with
\beta=0
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 1}|\varphi_{j}(z)||(E_{x_{j}}(F)-E_{x}(F))^{(\alpha)}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\,p_{K,\sigma_{3}}(F)\,\rho_{2}^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\rho_{2}(|z-x|+|z-x_{j}|))

\displaystyle\leq C_{4}\,p_{K,\sigma_{3}}(F)\,\rho_{2}^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\tfrac{7\rho_{2}}{b_{0}}|z-x|)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:rhotwo}}}{{\leq}}C_{4}\,p_{K,\sigma_{3}}(F)\,\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\tau_{1}|z-x|).

To estimate the terms with
\beta\neq 0
, let
y\in\partial A
be such that
|y-z|=d_{A}(z)
.
Then
\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j}^{(\beta)}(z)=0
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j}(z)\,(E_{x_{j}}(F)-E_{x}(F))^{(\alpha-\beta)}(z)=\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j}(z)\,(E_{x_{j}}(F)-E_{y}(F))^{(\alpha-\beta)}(z).

We have
|y-z|=d_{A}(z)\leq|z-x|\leq\frac{b_{0}}{7}
and thus
y\in H_{1}
.
Moreover, if
z\in Q_{j}^{*}
, then

|z-x_{j}|+|z-y|\leq\tfrac{6}{b_{0}}d_{A}(z)+d_{A}(z)\leq\tfrac{7}{b_{0}}d_{A}(z)\leq\tfrac{7}{b_{0}}|x-z|\leq 1.

Thus, by (
9.4
) and (
9.6
),

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq 1}|\varphi_{j}^{(\beta)}(z)||(E_{x_{j}}(F)-E_{y}(F))^{(\alpha-\beta)}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq 12^{2n}C_{1}C_{4}\,p_{K,\sigma_{3}}(F)\,\rho^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}\rho_{2}^{|\alpha-\beta|}M_{|\alpha-\beta|}\frac{h_{m}(\rho_{2}(|z-x_{j}|+|z-y|))}{h_{m}(\tau_{1}d_{A}(z))}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:rhotwo}}}{{\leq}}12^{2n}C_{1}C_{4}\,p_{K,\sigma_{3}}(F)\,\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}\frac{h_{m}(\frac{\tau_{1}}{C}d_{A}(z))}{h_{m}(\tau_{1}d_{A}(z))}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{hmgR}}}{{\leq}}12^{2n}C_{1}C_{4}\,p_{K,\sigma_{3}}(F)\,\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(\tau_{1}d_{A}(z)).

Then the claim follows easily.

We are ready to complete the proof of the proposition.
That
E(F)
is a
C^{\infty}
-function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
j^{\infty}_{A}E(F)=F
follows in analogy to the proof of
Theorem

8.4
.

It remains to show that
E(F)\in\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and that the linear map
E:\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)\to\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is continuous.
Let
H\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a compact convex set and
\rho>0
.
Let
H_{b_{0}/7}
be the closed
\tfrac{b_{0}}{7}
-neighborhood of
H
.
By the claim, there exist
C,\sigma,\tau>0
and a compact set
K\subseteq A
such that for all
x\in\partial A\cap H_{b_{0}/7}
,
z\in H_{b_{0}/7}\setminus A

with
|x-z|\leq\tfrac{b_{0}}{7}
,
F\in\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
we have (
9.2
), in particular,

(9.7)

|(E(F)-E_{x}(F))^{(\alpha)}(z)|\leq C\,p_{K,\sigma}(F)\,\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}.

If
z\in H\setminus A
satisfies
d_{A}(z)\leq\tfrac{b_{0}}{7}
, then there is
x\in\partial A\cap H_{b_{0}/7}
with
d_{A}(z)=|x-z|
.
For such
z
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

\displaystyle|E(F)^{(\alpha)}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq|E_{x}(F)^{(\alpha)}(z)|+|E(F)^{(\alpha)}(z)-E_{x}(F)^{(\alpha)}(z)|

\displaystyle\leq C\,p_{K,\sigma}(F)\,\rho^{|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|},

by (
9.7
) and by the assumption on local equicontinuity of
E_{x}
(increasing
K
,
C
, and
\sigma
if necessary).
Together with (
8.1
) this shows the assertion.
The proof is complete.
∎

9.2.
Extension operators always exist in the Beurling case

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
M
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a non-empty closed set.
There exists an extension operator
E:\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)\to\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

This follows from
Theorem

4.4
and
Proposition

9.2
.
∎

9.3.
A different approach based on a splitting theorem for Fréchet spaces

Let
M=(M_{k})
be a strongly regular sequence.
Let
K
and
L
be a non-empty compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
K\subseteq\operatorname{int}L
.
By
Theorem

8.6
, the sequence

(9.8)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{(M)}(L,K)~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{(M)}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\infty}_{K}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is an exact sequence of Fréchet spaces,
where we endow

\mathcal{D}^{(M)}(L,K):=\{f\in\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\operatorname{supp}f\subseteq L,\,j^{\infty}_{K}f=0\}

and
\mathcal{D}^{(M)}(L)

with the subspace topology of
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
By the splitting theorem of Vogt and Wagner
[
91
]
(see also
[
64
, 30.1]
),
the sequence splits if
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(K)
has property (DN) and

\mathcal{D}^{(M)}(L,K)

has property (
\Omega
).

A Fréchet space
E
with fundamental system of seminorms
\mathscr{P}=(p_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}

has
property
(DN) if

\;\exists m\in\mathbb{N}\;\forall k\in\mathbb{N}\;\exists\ell\in\mathbb{N}\;\exists C>0:p_{k}^{2}\leq Cp_{m}p_{\ell}.

Then
p_{m}
is a norm and every norm with this property is called a
dominating norm
.
It has
property
(
\Omega
) if

\;\forall m\in\mathbb{N}\;\exists k\in\mathbb{N}\;\forall\ell\in\mathbb{N}\;\exists C>0\;\exists\theta\in(0,1):p_{k}^{*}\leq C(p_{m}^{*})^{1-\theta}(p_{\ell}^{*})^{\theta},

where
p^{*}_{k}(y):=\sup\{|y(x)|:p_{k}(x)\leq 1\}
is the dual norm of
p_{k}
.
The properties (DN) and (
\Omega
) are linear topological invariants.
The property (DN) is inherited by all closed subspaces.
11
11

11

Hence, the splitting of
(
9.8
) implies that
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(K)
has the property (DN).

A nuclear Fréchet space
E
has property (DN) if and only if
E
is isomorphic to a closed subspace of
s
(the space of rapidly decreasing sequences).
The property (
\Omega
) is inherited by all quotient spaces.
A nuclear Fréchet space
E
has property (
\Omega
) if and only if
E
is isomorphic to a quotient space of
s
.
Cf.
[
64
, 29]
.

That
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(K)
has property (DN) is a triviality.
The fundamental system of seminorms

p_{m}(F):=\|F\|^{M}_{K,1/m}+|F|^{M}_{K,1/m},\quad m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1},

cf. (
8.3
) and (
8.4
), satisfies
p_{k}^{2}\leq p_{1}p_{k^{2}}
for all
k\geq 1
,
since

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{k^{|\alpha|}}{M_{|\alpha|}}\Big)^{2}=\frac{k^{2|\alpha|}}{M_{|\alpha|}}\frac{1^{|\alpha|}}{M_{|\alpha|}}.

That
\mathcal{D}^{(M)}(L,K)
has property (
\Omega
) follows from a result by Franken
[
37
]
(building on work of Meise and Taylor
[
63
]
).
Both papers are situated in the setting of Braun–Meise–Taylor classes of which the Denjoy–Carleman classes form a subclass if
M=(M_{k})
is a
strongly regular weight sequence; see
Example

12.3
(2).

9.4.
Continuous linear extension operators in the Roumieu case

As already pointed out in
Remark

4.5
in the case of the singleton, extension operators rarely exist in the Roumieu case.
For instance, the condition (
4.5
) due to
[
68
]

shows that
j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}:\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(\{0\})=\Lambda^{\{M\}}
has no continuous linear right-inverse
if
M
is any Gevrey sequence
M_{k}=G^{s}_{k}=k!^{s}
,
s>1
.

Actually,
j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}:\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}([-1,1]^{n})\to\Lambda^{\{M\}}
is not split surjective
for any strongly regular weight sequence
M
as seen by the following topological argument (cf.
[
51
, p.23]
):

\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}([-1,1]^{n})
is isomorphic to the dual of a power series space of finite type (cf.
[
52
]
),
which cannot have
\Lambda^{\{M\}}
as a complemented subspace, since
\Lambda^{\{M\}}
is isomorphic to the
dual of a power series space of infinite type (cf.
[
90
, p.269]
).

For the Whitney problem, Langenbruch
[
51
]
proved that
for compact convex subsets
K,L
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
\emptyset\neq\operatorname{int}K\subseteq K\subseteq\operatorname{int}L

there is no extension operator
E:\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(K)\to\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(L)
if

M
is a weight sequence satisfying

(9.9)

\displaystyle\;\exists a>0\;\forall b\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq a}:\overline{\lambda}(b):=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mu_{bk}}{\mu_{k}}\leq a\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mu_{bk}}{\mu_{k}}=:a\,\underline{\lambda}(b),

(9.10)

\displaystyle\lim_{b\to\infty}\frac{\underline{\lambda}(b)}{b}=\infty.

A weight sequence with these properties is strongly regular (cf.
[
51
, pp.355–356]

and
[
52
, Lemma 1.1]
). It is easy to check that the Gevrey sequences
G^{s}
,
s>1
, have the properties
(
9.9
) and (
9.10
) (indeed, in that case
\overline{\lambda}(b)=\underline{\lambda}(b)=b^{s}
).

9.5.
A different approach based on polynomial approximation

Denote by
\mathscr{P}_{d}
the space of all polynomials on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of degree at most
d
, and let
\mathscr{P}_{-1}:=\{0\}
.
For compact
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
continuous
f:K\to\mathbb{R}
we set

\operatorname{dist}_{K}(f,\mathscr{P}_{d}):=\inf\{\|f-p\|_{K}:p\in\mathscr{P}_{d}\}.

Let
M=(M_{k})
be a weight sequence.
Let
\mathcal{A}^{\{M\}}(K)
be the set of all
f\in C^{0}(K)
such that there exists
\rho>0
such that

(9.11)

\|f\|^{\mathcal{A}^{M}}_{K,\rho}:=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{d\geq-1}\frac{(d+2)^{k}\operatorname{dist}_{K}(f,\mathscr{P}_{d})}{\rho^{k}M_{k}}<\infty

and
\mathcal{A}^{(M)}(K)
the set of all
f\in C^{0}(K)
such that (
9.11
) holds for all
\rho>0
.
The spaces
\mathcal{A}^{[M]}(K)
are endowed with their natural locally convex topologies.

The following theorem is an ultradifferentiable version of Jackson’s theorem.

Theorem 9.4

(
[
71
, Theorem 2.7]
)
.

Let
M
be a derivation-closed weight sequence with
m_{k}^{1/k}\to\infty
.
For any compact
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have
j^{0}_{K}\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{[M]}(K)
, where
j^{0}_{K}(f)=f|_{K}
.
The inclusion is continuous.

Let
r\geq 1
.
A compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is said to have the
Markov property

(P_{r})
if
there exist
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that
for every polynomial
p
12
12

12

It is not hard to see that
(P_{r})
holds if and only if there is
C>0
such that

\|p^{(\alpha)}\|_{K}\leq C\deg(p)^{r|\alpha|}\|p\|_{K}
for all
|\alpha|\geq 1
and all polynomials
p
;
cf.
[
70
, Theorem 3.3]
.
Any compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that is uniformly polynomially cuspidal has the Markov property,
in particular, any fat subanalytic compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(9.12)

|p(z)|\leq C_{1}\|p\|_{K}\quad\text{ for }z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\text{ with }\operatorname{dist}(z,K)\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\deg(p)^{r}}.

If
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies
(P_{r})
, then necessarily
r\geq 2
, by
[
5
]
,
and
K
is
C^{\infty}
-determining
, see
[
70
, Remark 3.5]
,
that means
j^{0}_{K}f=0
implies
j^{\infty}_{K}f=0
for any
C^{\infty}
-function
f
.

The following extension theorem involves an unavoidable loss of regularity: for instance

[
39
]
shows that
j^{0}_{[-1,1]}:\mathcal{E}^{(G^{s})}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{A}^{(G^{s})}([-1,1])
is not surjective.
On the other hand the weight sequence is not required to be strongly non-quasianalytic.

Theorem 9.5

(
[
71
]
)
.

Let
M
be a non-quasianalytic weight sequence of moderate growth.
Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a compact set satisfying
(P_{r})
with some
r\geq 2
.
Then there exists a continuous linear operator

L:\mathcal{A}^{[M]}(K)\to\mathcal{E}^{[M^{r+1}]}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that
j^{0}_{K}\circ L=\operatorname{id}
. Here
M^{r+1}:=(M^{r+1}_{k})
is the
(r+1)
-st component-wise power of
M
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.11
, there is a sequence
(\varphi_{k})_{k\geq 1}
of functions
\varphi_{k}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that
0\leq\varphi_{k}\leq 1
,
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{k}\subseteq U_{k}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:d_{K}(x)<C_{2}k^{-r}\}

(where
C_{2}
is the constant from (
9.12
)),

\varphi_{k}=1
in a neighborhood of
K
, and

|\varphi_{k}^{(\alpha)}|\leq C(n)^{|\alpha|}k^{r|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|},\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}.

For
f\in\mathcal{A}^{[M]}(K)
let
L_{d}f
be the Lagrange interpolation polynomial of
f
with
nodes in Fekete–Leja extremal points of
K
of order
d
; cf.
[
70
, Section 2]
.
Then

\|f-L_{d}f\|_{K}\leq 2(d+1)^{n}\operatorname{dist}_{K}(f,\mathscr{P}_{d}).

Set

Lf(x):=\varphi_{1}(x)L_{1}f(x)+\sum_{d=1}^{\infty}\varphi_{d}(x)(L_{d+1}f(x)-L_{d}f(x))

and let us check that
L
is well-defined and has the desired properties.
By (
9.12
),

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}

\displaystyle(\varphi_{d}(x)(L_{d+1}f(x)-L_{d}f(x)))|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}|\varphi_{d}^{(\beta)}(x)||(L_{d+1}f-L_{d}f)^{(\alpha-\beta)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\|\varphi_{d}^{(\beta)}\|_{U_{d}}\|(L_{d+1}f-L_{d}f)^{(\alpha-\beta)}\|_{K}

\displaystyle\leq CC_{1}\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}C(n)^{|\beta|}d^{r|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}\cdot(d+2)^{n+r|\alpha-\beta|}\operatorname{dist}_{K}(f,\mathscr{P}_{d})

\displaystyle\leq D\sigma^{|\alpha|}(d+2)^{n+r|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}\operatorname{dist}_{K}(f,\mathscr{P}_{d}),

for suitable constants
D,\sigma>0
.
Thus, by (
9.11
) and moderate growth of
M
,

\displaystyle\|\partial^{\alpha}(\varphi_{d}(L_{d+1}f-L_{d}f))\|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq D\sigma^{|\alpha|}(d+2)^{n+r|\alpha|}M_{|\alpha|}\cdot\|f\|^{\mathcal{A}^{M}}_{K,\rho}\frac{\rho^{n+2+r|\alpha|}M_{n+2+r|\alpha|}}{(d+2)^{n+2+r|\alpha|}}

\displaystyle\leq D_{1}\sigma_{1}^{|\alpha|}M^{r+1}_{|\alpha|}\|f\|^{\mathcal{A}^{M}}_{K,\rho}\cdot\frac{1}{(d+2)^{2}}.

It follows that
Lf
is a well-defined
C^{\infty}
-function satisfying

\|(Lf)^{(\alpha)}\|^{M^{r+1}}_{\mathbb{R}^{n},\sigma_{1}}\leq D_{2}\,\|f\|^{\mathcal{A}^{M}}_{K,\rho},

which implies the assertion; notice for the Beurling case that
\sigma_{1}\to 0
as
\rho\to 0
.
∎

10.
Extension with controlled loss of regularity

If the weight sequence
M=(M_{k})
is not strongly non-quasianalytic, and thus extension preserving the class is not possible,
it is natural to ask if the loss of regularity occurring in the extension can at least be controlled.
This is indeed the case.
It requires a new extension technique which is inspired by Dyn’kin’s theory of
almost analytic functions
;
cf.
[
33
,
34
]
and also
[
40
]
.
For the singleton see
Theorem

6.2
.

10.1.
The setup: admissible pairs of weight sequences

Let
M,N
be strongly log-convex weight sequences,
M
of moderate growth with
m_{k}^{1/k}\to\infty
,
N
non-quasianalytic with

M\preccurlyeq N
, and

(10.1)

\sup_{k}\frac{\mu_{k}}{k}\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{1}{\nu_{j}}<\infty,

where
\mu_{k}=\tfrac{M_{k}}{M_{k-1}}
and
\nu_{k}=\tfrac{N_{k}}{N_{k-1}}
.
In that case we call
(M,N)
an
admissible pair
.
Note that
(M,M)
is an admissible pair if and only if
M
is strongly regular.
Recall that the conditions (
6.1
) and (
10.1
) are equivalent
if
M
has moderate growth; so our definition is compatible with the case treated in
Theorem

6.2
.

Remark 10.1
.

Let
N
be a strongly log-convex non-quasianalytic weight sequence of moderate growth
and let
S
be the descendant of
N
.
Then
(S,N)
is an admissible pair
and, if
(M,N)
is another admissible pair, then
M\preccurlyeq S
; see
Lemma

4.7
.
Note that, if
M
has moderate growth, then
M\preccurlyeq N
if and only if
\mu\lesssim\nu
,
indeed,

\mu_{k}\lesssim M_{k}^{1/k}\lesssim N_{k}^{1/k}\leq\nu_{k}
.

10.2.
Suitable cutoff functions

Let
(M,N)
be an admissible pair of weight sequences.
In analogy to the construction of optimal cutoff functions in
Section

7.3

we obtain cutoff functions with bounds reflecting the weaker assumption (
10.1
).

Proposition 10.2

(
[
27
, Proposition 4]
)
.

Let
(M,N)
be an admissible pair of weight sequences.
There is a constant
C>0
such that for all
\rho,r,\epsilon>0
there exists
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}^{\{N\}}(\mathbb{R})

with
\varphi|_{[-r,r]}=1
,
\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subseteq[-(1+\epsilon)r,(1+\epsilon)r]
, and

\|\varphi\|^{N}_{\mathbb{R},\rho}\leq\frac{1}{h_{m}(C\rho r\epsilon)}.

Then it is straightforward to construct a partition of unity subordinate to a given family of Whitney cubes.

Proposition 10.3

(
[
27
, Proposition 6]
)
.

Let
(M,N)
be an admissible pair of weight sequences.
Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a non-empty compact set and
(Q_{j})_{j\geq 1}
a family of Whitney cubes for
K
.
Then there exists

C_{1}\geq 1
such that for all
\epsilon>0
there is a family of
C^{\infty}
-functions

(\varphi_{j,\epsilon})_{j\geq 1}
satisfying

(1)

0\leq\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\leq 1
for all
j\geq 1
,

(2)

\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\subseteq Q^{*}_{j}
for all
j\geq 1
,

(3)

\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}(x)=1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K
,

(4)

for all
j\geq 1
,
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K
,

|\varphi^{(\beta)}_{j,\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{\epsilon^{|\beta|}N_{|\beta|}}{h_{m}(C_{1}\epsilon\,d_{K}(x))}.

10.3.
Extension of Whitney ultrajets

Theorem 10.4

(
[
27
]
)
.

Let
M,N
be strongly log-convex weight sequences,
M
of moderate growth with
m_{k}^{1/k}\to\infty
, and
N
non-quasianalytic with

M\preccurlyeq N
. Then
(M,N)
is admissible if and only if

\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(A)\subseteq j^{\infty}_{A}\mathcal{E}^{\{N\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all closed
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We will sketch the proof of the “only if” part.
Assume that
(M,N)
is admissible.
The new feature is the shape of the extension formula:
Let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be compact
and
F=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(K)
satisfy (
8.1
) and (
8.2
).
Let
(Q_{j})_{j\geq 1}
be a family of Whitney cubes for
K
.
Let
\epsilon,L>0
be fixed.
There is a family of
C^{\infty}
-functions
(\varphi_{j,\epsilon})_{j\geq 1}
satisfying
the conclusion of
Proposition

10.3
.
Let
x_{j}
be the center of
Q_{j}
and let

\hat{x}_{j}\in K
be such that
|x_{j}-\hat{x}_{j}|=d_{K}(x_{j})
. We define

(10.2)

f(z):=\begin{cases}\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}(z)\,T^{2\Gamma_{m}(Ld_{K}(x_{j}))}_{\hat{x}_{j}}F(z),&\text{ if }z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K,\\
F^{0}(z),&\text{ if }z\in K.\end{cases}

(So the local extensions
f_{\hat{x}_{j}}
in (
8.9
) are replaced by the Taylor polynomials
T^{2\Gamma_{m}(Ld_{K}(x_{j}))}_{\hat{x}_{j}}F
.)
The degree
p(x):=2\Gamma_{m}(Ld_{K}(x))
of the Taylor approximation tends to infinity as
x
approaches the set
K
;
the function
\Gamma_{m}
was introduce in
Section

7.2
.

The proof that (
10.2
) is indeed the required extension
is similar to the proof of
Theorem

8.4
.
First we need estimates for the partial derivatives of the Taylor polynomials
T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F
.

Lemma 10.5
.

There is a constant
A_{0}=A_{0}(M,N)>1
such that
for all
F\in\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(K)
satisfying (
8.1
) and (
8.2
) (with the constants
C,\rho
),
all
L\geq A_{0}\rho
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

(10.3)

\displaystyle|(T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C(2L)^{|\alpha|+1}M_{|\alpha|},

and, if
|\alpha|<p(x)
,

(10.4)

\displaystyle|(T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)-F^{\alpha}(\hat{x})|

\displaystyle\leq C(2L)^{|\alpha|+1}|\alpha|!\,m_{|\alpha|+1}d_{K}(x).

Proof.

For (
10.3
) we may restrict to the case
|\alpha|\leq p(x)
. By (
8.1
),

\displaystyle|(T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\leq\beta\\
|\beta|\leq p(x)\end{subarray}}\frac{|x-\hat{x}|^{|\beta|-|\alpha|}}{(\beta-\alpha)!}C\rho^{|\beta|}M_{|\beta|}

\displaystyle\leq C|\alpha|!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\leq\beta\\
|\beta|\leq p(x)\end{subarray}}\frac{|\beta|!\,(nd_{K}(x))^{|\beta|-|\alpha|}}{|\alpha|!\,(|\beta|-|\alpha|)!}\rho^{|\beta|}m_{|\beta|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C|\alpha|!}{(nd_{K}(x))^{|\alpha|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\leq\beta\\
|\beta|\leq p(x)\end{subarray}}(2n\rho d_{K}(x))^{|\beta|}m_{|\beta|}

(10.5)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C|\alpha|!}{(nd_{K}(x))^{|\alpha|}}\sum_{j=|\alpha|}^{p(x)}(2n^{2}\rho d_{K}(x))^{j}m_{j},

since the number of
\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
|\beta|=j
is bounded by
n^{j}
.
By Lemma
7.2
,

(10.6)

\text{there exists $\lambda<1$ such that $2\Gamma_{m}(t)\leq\Gamma_{m}(\lambda t)$ for all $t>0$},

and thus

\displaystyle|(T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C|\alpha|!}{(nd_{K}(x))^{|\alpha|}}\sum_{j=|\alpha|}^{\Gamma_{m}(L\lambda d_{K}(x))}(2n^{2}\rho d_{K}(x))^{j}m_{j}.

Since
(L\lambda d_{K}(x))^{j}m_{j}\leq(L\lambda d_{K}(x))^{|\alpha|}m_{|\alpha|}
for
|\alpha|\leq j\leq\Gamma_{m}(L\lambda d_{K}(x))
, by (
7.5
),

\displaystyle|(T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq CM_{|\alpha|}\Big(\frac{L\lambda}{n}\Big)^{|\alpha|}\sum_{j=|\alpha|}^{\Gamma_{m}(L\lambda d_{K}(x))}\Big(\frac{2n^{2}\rho}{L\lambda}\Big)^{j}.

We obtain (
10.3
) if
L
is chosen such that
\frac{2n^{2}\rho}{L\lambda}\leq\frac{1}{2}
;
then
A_{0}=\frac{4n^{2}}{\lambda}
.

For (
10.4
) it suffices to note that, if
|\alpha|<p(x)
,

(T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)-F^{\alpha}(\hat{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\leq\beta\\
|\alpha|<|\beta|\leq p(x)\end{subarray}}\frac{(x-\hat{x})^{\beta-\alpha}}{(\beta-\alpha)!}F^{\beta}(\hat{x}),

and to follow the same arguments.
∎

The core of the proof is the following lemma.

Lemma 10.6
.

There exist constants
A_{i}=A_{i}(M,N)
,
i=1,\ldots,4
,
such that the following holds.
If
\epsilon=A_{1}L
and
L>A_{2}\rho
,
then for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K
with
d_{K}(x)<1
and all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
we have

(10.7)

|(f-T^{p(x)}_{\hat{x}}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)|\leq C(LA_{3})^{|\alpha|+1}N_{|\alpha|}h_{m}(LA_{4}\,d_{K}(x)).

Here
C,\rho
are the constants from (
8.1
) and (
8.2
).

Proof.

By the Leibniz rule we have

(f-T^{p(x)}_{\hat{x}}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)=\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\binom{\alpha}{\beta}\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{(\alpha-\beta)}(x)\big(T^{p(x_{j})}_{\hat{x}_{j}}F-T^{p(x)}_{\hat{x}}F\big)^{(\beta)}(x).

Let us estimate

\displaystyle|\big(T^{p(x_{j})}_{\hat{x}_{j}}F-T^{p(x)}_{\hat{x}}F\big)^{(\beta)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|\big(T^{p(x_{j})}_{\hat{x}_{j}}F-T^{p(x_{j})}_{\hat{x}}F\big)^{(\beta)}(x)|+|\big(T^{p(x_{j})}_{\hat{x}}F-T^{p(x)}_{\hat{x}}F\big)^{(\beta)}(x)|=:H_{1}+H_{2}

for
x\in Q_{j}^{*}
(since
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}\subseteq Q_{j}^{*}
).
One checks easily (as in the proof of
Lemma

8.3
) that, with
2q:=p(x_{j})
,

H_{1}\leq C(2n^{2}\rho)^{2q+1}|\beta|!\,m_{2q+1}(|\hat{x}_{j}-x|+|\hat{x}_{j}-\hat{x}|)^{2q+1-|\beta|}.

By
Proposition

7.7
, there exist universal constants
0<b\leq 1\leq B
such that

(10.8)

b\,d_{K}(x)\leq d_{K}(x_{j})\leq B\,d_{K}(x),\quad\text{ for }x\in Q^{*}_{j}.

Moreover,
|\hat{x}_{j}-x|+|\hat{x}_{j}-\hat{x}|\lesssim d_{K}(x_{j})
. So, using that
m
has moderate growth,

H_{1}\leq C(D_{1}\rho)^{2q+1}|\beta|!\,m_{q}^{2}(D_{2}\,d_{K}(x_{j}))^{2q+1-|\beta|},

for constants
D_{i}\geq 1
which depend only on
M
,
N
, and the dimension
n
.
Since
h_{m}(Ld_{K}(x_{j}))=m_{q}(Ld_{K}(x_{j}))^{q}\leq m_{|\beta|}(Ld_{K}(x_{j}))^{|\beta|}
, by (
7.6
),
we get

H_{1}\leq CD_{1}D_{2}\rho\Big(\frac{D_{1}D_{2}\rho}{L}\Big)^{2q}d_{K}(x_{j})|\beta|!\,m_{|\beta|}L^{|\beta|}h_{m}(Ld_{K}(x_{j})).

Since
d_{K}(x_{j})\lesssim d_{K}(x)<1
(by assumption), there is
D_{3}
such that for
L>D_{3}\rho
,

H_{1}\leq CL^{|\beta|+1}M_{|\beta|}h_{m}(Ld_{K}(x_{j})).

Now consider
H_{2}
.
By (
10.6
) and (
10.8
),
we have

p(x_{j})=2\Gamma_{m}(Ld_{K}(x_{j}))\leq 2\Gamma_{m}(Lbd_{K}(x))\leq\Gamma_{m}(Lb\lambda d_{K}(x))

and similarly
p(x)\leq\Gamma_{m}(Lb\lambda d_{K}(x))
. So the polynomial
T^{p(x_{j})}_{\hat{x}}F-T^{p(x)}_{\hat{x}}F

has degree at most
\Gamma_{m}(Lb\lambda d_{K}(x))
.
On the other hand, again by (
10.8
), the valuation of the polynomial
T^{p(x_{j})}_{\hat{x}}F-T^{p(x)}_{\hat{x}}F
is at least

2\Gamma_{m}(LBd_{K}(x))=:2r
.
Thus, the calculation (
10.5
) gives

\displaystyle H_{2}\leq\frac{C|\beta|!}{(nd_{K}(x))^{|\beta|}}\sum_{j=2r}^{\Gamma_{m}(Lb\lambda d_{K}(x))}(2n^{2}\rho d_{K}(x))^{j}m_{j}.

For such
j
we have
m_{j}(Lb\lambda d_{K}(x))^{j}\leq m_{2r}(Lb\lambda d_{K}(x))^{2r}
, by (
7.5
),
and
h_{m}(LBd_{K}(x))=m_{r}(LBd_{K}(x))^{r}\leq m_{|\beta|}(LBd_{K}(x))^{|\beta|}
, by (
7.6
).
Using moderate growth of
m
, we may thus conclude that there is
D_{4}\geq 1
such that, for
L>D_{4}\rho
,

\displaystyle H_{2}

\displaystyle\leq CL^{|\beta|+1}M_{|\beta|}h_{m}(LBd_{K}(x)).

In summary, for
x\in Q_{j}^{*}
and
d_{K}(x)<1
,

\displaystyle|\big(T^{p(x_{j})}_{\hat{x}_{j}}F-T^{p(x)}_{\hat{x}}F\big)^{(\beta)}(x)|\leq C(2L)^{|\beta|+1}M_{|\beta|}h_{m}(LBd_{K}(x))

and, consequently, in view of
Proposition

10.3
,

\displaystyle|(f-T^{p(x)}_{\hat{x}}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}\frac{\alpha!}{\beta!(\alpha-\beta)!}\cdot 12^{2n}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{|\alpha|-|\beta|}N_{|\alpha|-|\beta|}}{h_{m}(C_{1}\epsilon\,d_{K}(x))}\cdot C(2L)^{|\beta|+1}M_{|\beta|}\,h_{m}(LBd_{K}(x))

\displaystyle\leq C12^{2n}\sum_{j=0}^{|\alpha|}\frac{|\alpha|!\,n^{|\alpha|+j}}{j!(|\alpha|-j)!}\epsilon^{|\alpha|-j}(2L)^{j+1}N_{|\alpha|-j}M_{j}\,\frac{h_{m}(LBd_{K}(x))}{h_{m}(C_{1}\epsilon\,d_{K}(x))}.

Now, by (
7.9
),

\frac{h_{m}(LBd_{K}(x))}{h_{m}(C_{1}\epsilon\,d_{K}(x))}\leq h_{m}(LBCd_{K}(x))

if we choose
\epsilon:=\frac{LBC}{C_{1}}
, where
C
is the constant from (
7.9
).
Noting that
M\preccurlyeq N
, the lemma follows.
∎

Now we are ready to check that
(
10.2
) is the desired extension of
F
.
That
f
defines a
C^{\infty}
-function on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
j^{\infty}_{K}f=F
can be seen as follows.
Let us fix a point
a\in K
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
.
Since
\Gamma_{m}(t)\to\infty
as
t\to 0
, we have
|\alpha|<p(x)=2\Gamma_{m}(Ld_{K}(x))
if
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K
is
sufficiently close to
a
. Thus, as
x\to a
,

\displaystyle|f^{(\alpha)}(x)-F^{\alpha}(a)|

\displaystyle\leq|(f-T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)|+|(T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)-F^{\alpha}(\hat{x})|+|F^{\alpha}(\hat{x})-F^{\alpha}(a)|

\displaystyle=O(h_{m}(LA_{4}d_{K}(x)))+O(d_{K}(x))+O(|\hat{x}-a|),

by (
8.2
), (
10.4
), and (
10.7
).
Hence
f^{(\alpha)}(x)\to F^{\alpha}(a)
as
x\to a
.

To get an extension in
\mathcal{E}^{\{N\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we first observe that,
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K
with
d_{K}(x)<1
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, and a suitable constant
A=A(n,M,N)
,

\displaystyle|f^{(\alpha)}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq|(T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)|+|f^{(\alpha)}(x)-(T_{\hat{x}}^{p(x)}F)^{(\alpha)}(x)|\leq C(LA)^{|\alpha|+1}N_{|\alpha|}

by (
10.3
) and (
10.7
),
because
h_{m}\leq 1
. In view of (
8.1
),
it suffices to multiply
f
with a
suitable cutoff function of class
\mathcal{E}^{\{N\}}
with support in
\{x:d_{K}(x)<1\}

in order to obtain an extension in
\mathcal{E}^{\{N\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Remark 10.7
.

The proof shows that for each
\rho>0
there is an extension operator

\mathcal{E}^{M}_{\rho}(K)\to\mathcal{E}^{N}_{A\rho}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for a suitable constant
A
, but the operator depends
on
\rho
(through
L
and
\epsilon
).

There is a corresponding result in the Beurling case.

Theorem 10.8

(
[
27
]
)
.

Let
M,N
be strongly log-convex weight sequences,
M
of moderate growth with
m_{k}^{1/k}\to\infty
,
and
N
non-quasianalytic with

M\preccurlyeq N
. Then
(M,N)
is admissible if and only if

\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)\subseteq j^{\infty}_{A}\mathcal{E}^{(N)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all closed
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Let
(M,N)
be a pair of admissible weight sequences and let
L
be a non-negative sequence such that
L\lhd M
.
Then there exists an admissible pair of weight sequences
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
such that
L\preccurlyeq M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}\lhd N
.
Indeed, as in the proof of
Lemma

8.5
there is a decreasing sequence
(\epsilon_{k})_{k\geq 1}
tending to
0

with
L_{k}\leq\epsilon_{1}\cdots\epsilon_{k}M_{k}
for all
k
.
Applying
Lemma

3.4
to
\alpha_{k}=0
,
\beta_{k}=\max\{\epsilon_{k},\frac{k}{\mu_{k}}\}
, and
\gamma_{k}=\frac{k}{\mu_{k}}
,
gives a sequence
(\theta_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that
\theta_{k}\nearrow\infty
,
\frac{k\theta_{k}}{\mu_{k}}\searrow 0
and
\theta_{k}\epsilon_{k}\to 0
.
Applying
Lemma

3.4
once more, now to
\alpha_{k}=\frac{1}{\nu_{k}}
,
\beta_{k}=\max\{\frac{1}{\theta_{k}},\frac{k}{\nu_{k}}\}
, and
\gamma_{k}=\frac{k}{\nu_{k}}
,
yields a sequence
(\vartheta_{k})_{k\geq 1}

with
\vartheta_{k}\nearrow\infty

such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{\vartheta_{j}}{\nu_{j}}\leq 8\vartheta_{k}\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{1}{\nu_{j}},\quad\text{ for all }k\geq 1,

\frac{\vartheta_{k}}{\theta_{k}}\to 0
, and
\frac{k\vartheta_{k}}{\nu_{k}}\searrow 0
.
Define

\mu^{\prime}_{k}:=\frac{\mu_{k}}{\theta_{k}}
and
\nu^{\prime}_{k}:=\frac{\nu_{k}}{\vartheta_{k}}
for
k\geq 1
and
\mu^{\prime}_{0}=\nu^{\prime}_{0}:=1
.
Then
\frac{\mu^{\prime}_{k}}{k}\nearrow\infty
and
\frac{\nu^{\prime}_{k}}{k}\nearrow\infty
and hence

M^{\prime}_{k}:=\mu^{\prime}_{0}\mu^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\mu^{\prime}_{k}
and
N^{\prime}_{k}:=\nu^{\prime}_{0}\nu^{\prime}_{1}\cdots\nu^{\prime}_{k}
are strongly log-convex weight sequences
such that
(m^{\prime}_{k})^{1/k}\to\infty
and
(n^{\prime}_{k})^{1/k}\to\infty
.
That
M^{\prime}
has moderate growth follows from

\frac{M^{\prime}_{j+k}}{M^{\prime}_{j}M^{\prime}_{k}}=\frac{M_{j+k}}{M_{j}M_{k}}\frac{\theta_{1}\cdots\theta_{j}\theta_{1}\cdots\theta_{k}}{\theta_{1}\cdots\theta_{j+k}}\leq\frac{M_{j+k}}{M_{j}M_{k}}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{1}{\nu^{\prime}}=\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{\vartheta_{j}}{\nu_{j}}\leq 8\vartheta_{k}\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{1}{\nu_{j}}\lesssim\frac{k\vartheta_{k}}{\mu_{k}}\lesssim\frac{k\theta_{k}}{\mu_{k}}=\frac{k}{\mu_{k}^{\prime}}

and hence
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is an admissible pair.
We have
L\preccurlyeq M^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}\lhd N
, since

(\frac{L_{k}}{M^{\prime}_{k}})^{1/k}=(\frac{L_{k}}{M_{k}})^{1/k}(\theta_{1}\cdots\theta_{k})^{1/k}\leq(\theta_{1}\epsilon_{1}\cdots\theta_{k}\epsilon_{k})^{1/k}
is bounded
and
(\frac{N_{k}}{N^{\prime}_{k}})^{1/k}=(\vartheta_{1}\cdots\vartheta_{k})^{1/k}\to\infty
.

Then it is easy to conclude
\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(A)\subseteq j^{\infty}_{A}\mathcal{E}^{(N)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all closed
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
by a reduction argument to the Roumieu case (similar to the proof of
Theorem

8.6
).

10.4.
Extension operator

Concerning the existence of extension operators in the mixed setting we have

Theorem 10.9

(
[
27
]
)
.

Let
M,N
be strongly log-convex weight sequences,
M
of moderate growth with
m_{k}^{1/k}\to\infty
,
and
N
non-quasianalytic with
M\lhd N
.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mu_{k}}{k}\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{1}{\nu_{j}}=0
.

(2)

\Lambda^{\{M\}}\subseteq j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}\mathcal{E}^{(N)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(3)

There exists an extension operator
\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(A)\to\mathcal{E}^{(N)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for each closed non-empty subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We briefly comment on the implication (1)
\Rightarrow
(3).
The idea is to show that (1) implies the existence of
an admissible pair
(M^{\prime},N^{\prime})
of weight sequences such that

M\lhd M^{\prime}\lhd N^{\prime}\lhd N
. This can be achieved again with the help of
Lemma

3.4
; for the details we refer to

[
27
, Proposition 20]
.
Then the extension operator is given by the composite

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(K)~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{M^{\prime}}_{1}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{N^{\prime}}_{A}(\mathbb{R}^{n})~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{(N)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

where the middle arrow is the extension operator from
Remark

10.7
.

Remark 10.10
.

All extensions in this section can be chosen to be analytic in the complement of
A

in view of
Theorem

8.9
.

Part II
More general ultradifferentiable classes

11.
Further ultradifferentiable functions

We introduce weight functions and Braun–Meise–Taylor classes.
This quickly leads to the notion of the associated weight matrix
which allows us to build the theory of Braun–Meise–Taylor classes (and of more general ultradifferentiable classes) to a great extent upon the theory of
Denjoy–Carleman classes.

11.1.
Weight functions

Definition 11.1
.

A
weight function
is a continuous increasing function

\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\omega(0)=0
satisfying the following three properties:

(1)

\omega(2t)=O(\omega(t))
as
t\to\infty
.

(2)

\log(t)=o(\omega(t))
as
t\to\infty
.

(3)

\varphi:t\mapsto\omega(e^{t})
is convex on
[0,\infty)
.

Note that (2) is equivalent to
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t}{\varphi(t)}=0
and implies
\lim_{t\to\infty}\omega(t)=\infty
.
For each weight function
\omega
there is a weight function
\tilde{\omega}
which coincides with
\omega
for all sufficiently large
t>0
and such that
\tilde{\omega}|_{[0,1]}=0
.
It is hence no loss of generality to assume that
\omega|_{[0,1]}=0
which we shall do
tacitly if convenient.

Since
\varphi
is convex, increasing, and
\varphi(0)=0
, we may consider the
Young conjugate

\varphi^{*}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)

of
\varphi
,
given by

\varphi^{*}(t):=\sup_{s\geq 0}(st-\varphi(s)),\quad t\geq 0.

The function
\varphi^{*}
is convex, increasing, and
it satisfies
\varphi^{*}(0)=0
,
\varphi^{**}=\varphi
(here we use
\omega|_{[0,1]}=0
),
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{t}{\varphi^{*}(t)}=0
.
Moreover,
the functions
t\mapsto\tfrac{\varphi(t)}{t}
and
t\mapsto\tfrac{\varphi^{*}(t)}{t}
are increasing on
(0,\infty)
.

11.2.
Braun–Meise–Taylor classes

Let
\omega
be a weight function.
Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\rho>0
.
For
f\in C^{\infty}(U)
and compact
K\subseteq U
we consider
the seminorm

\|f\|^{\omega}_{K,\rho}:=\sup_{x\in K}\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}|f^{(\alpha)}(x)|\exp(-\tfrac{1}{\rho}\varphi^{*}(\rho|\alpha|)).

(Occasionally, we will also use
\|f\|^{\omega}_{U,\rho}
for open sets
U
.)
We define the
Braun–Meise–Taylor classes

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)

\displaystyle:=\big\{f\in C^{\infty}(U):\forall K\subseteq_{cp}U~\forall\rho>0:\|f\|^{\omega}_{K,\rho}<\infty\big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)

\displaystyle:=\big\{f\in C^{\infty}(U):\forall K\subseteq_{cp}U~\exists\rho>0:\|f\|^{\omega}_{K,\rho}<\infty\big\}.

The class
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)
is said to be of
Beurling type

and the class
\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)
of
Roumieu type
.
In analogy to Denjoy–Carleman classes we use
\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)
as placeholder for either
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)

or
\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)
; cf.
2.1
.

We endow the vector spaces
\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)
with their natural locally convex topologies:

\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)
is supplied with its natural Fréchet space topology and

\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)
with
the projective limit topology over
K
of the
inductive limit topology over
\rho
; note that it suffices to take countable limits.
Then
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)
is a nuclear Fréchet space and

\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)
is nuclear, complete, and reflexive; see

[
19
, Proposition 4.9]
.

Remark 11.2
.

The classes
\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}
originate from work of Beurling
[
7
]
(see also Björck
[
10
]
).
Beurling described his non-quasianalytic classes by decay properties of the Fourier transform of a compactly supported function:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(U)

\displaystyle=\Big\{f\in C_{c}(U):~\forall\rho>0:\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\widehat{f}(\xi)|e^{\rho\omega(\xi)}\,dt<\infty\Big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\{\omega\}}(U)

\displaystyle=\Big\{f\in C_{c}(U):~\exists\rho>0:\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\widehat{f}(\xi)|e^{\rho\omega(\xi)}\,dt<\infty\Big\}.

Here, and below,
\omega(\xi):=\omega(|\xi|)
.
Braun, Meise, and Taylor
[
19
]
showed that these classes are non-trivial (for each non-empty open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
) provided
that
\omega
is a weight function (not necessarily satisfying
11.1
(3))
13
13

13

Beurling and Björck had additionally assumed that
\omega
is subadditive.

such that

(11.1)

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(t)}{t^{2}}\,dt<\infty.

We will give an independent proof of this fact in

Theorem

11.17
.
Furthermore, if also the condition
11.1
(3) is fulfilled, then
the classes
\mathcal{D}^{[\omega]}
also admit the description

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(U)

\displaystyle=\big\{f\in\mathcal{D}(U):~\forall\rho>0:\|f\|^{\omega}_{U,\rho}<\infty\big\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\{\omega\}}(U)

\displaystyle=\big\{f\in\mathcal{D}(U):~\exists\rho>0:\|f\|^{\omega}_{U,\rho}<\infty\big\}.

This was the starting point for the introduction of the local classes
\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)

and the development of a corresponding theory of ultradistributions
in
[
19
]
.

11.3.
Associated weight matrix

We associate with a weight function
\omega
a one-parameter family
\mathfrak{W}=\{W^{x}\}_{x>0}

of weight sequences
W^{x}=(W^{x}_{k})
defined by

W^{x}_{k}:=\exp(\tfrac{1}{x}\varphi^{*}(xk)),\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

The family
\mathfrak{W}
is called the
associated weight matrix
of
\omega
.
As usual for weight sequences, we write

W^{x}_{k}=\vartheta^{x}_{0}\vartheta^{x}_{1}\vartheta^{x}_{2}\cdots\vartheta^{x}_{k}=k!\,w^{x}_{k}.

Notice that, for any
\rho>0
,

(11.2)

\|f\|^{\omega}_{K,\rho}=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{\|f^{(\alpha)}\|_{K}}{\exp(\tfrac{1}{\rho}\varphi^{*}(\rho|\alpha|))}=\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\frac{\|f^{(\alpha)}\|_{K}}{W^{\rho}_{|\alpha|}}=\|f\|^{W^{\rho}}_{K,1}.

Lemma 11.3
.

We have:

(1)

Each
W^{x}
is a weight sequence.

(2)

\vartheta^{x}\leq\vartheta^{y}
if
x\leq y
, which entails
W^{x}\leq W^{y}
.

(3)

For all
x>0
and all
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
W^{x}_{j+k}\leq W^{2x}_{j}W^{2x}_{k}
and
w^{x}_{j+k}\leq w^{2x}_{j}w^{2x}_{k}
.

(4)

For all
x>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
,
\vartheta^{x}_{2k}\leq\vartheta^{4x}_{k}
.

(5)

\forall\rho>0~\exists H\geq 1~\forall x>0~\exists C\geq 1~\forall k\in\mathbb{N}:\rho^{k}W^{x}_{k}\leq CW^{Hx}_{k}
.

Proof.

(1)
The convexity of
\varphi^{*}
implies that each
\vartheta^{x}
is increasing.
And

(W^{x}_{k})^{1/k}\nearrow\infty

follows from

t\mapsto\frac{\varphi^{*}(t)}{t}\nearrow\infty

(2) Let
0<x\leq y
. Then

\vartheta^{x}_{k}=\exp\Big(\frac{\varphi^{*}(xk)-\varphi^{*}(x(k-1))}{x}\Big)\leq\exp\Big(\frac{\varphi^{*}(yk)-\varphi^{*}(y(k-1))}{y}\Big)=\vartheta^{y}_{k}

again by the convexity of
\varphi^{*}
.

(3) The convexity of
\varphi^{*}
and
\varphi^{*}(0)=0
implies

\varphi^{*}(t)+\varphi^{*}(s)\leq\varphi^{*}(t+s)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi^{*}(2t)+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi^{*}(2s),\quad t,s\geq 0.

As a consequence

W^{x}_{j}W^{x}_{k}\leq W^{x}_{j+k}\leq W^{2x}_{j}W^{2x}_{k},\quad j,k\in\mathbb{N},

and equivalently

w^{x}_{j}w^{x}_{k}\leq\binom{j+k}{j}w^{x}_{j+k}\leq w^{2x}_{j}w^{2x}_{k},\quad j,k\in\mathbb{N},

(4) The inequality
\vartheta^{x}_{2k}\leq\vartheta^{4x}_{k}
is equivalent to

\frac{\varphi^{*}(2kx)-\varphi^{*}((2k-1)x)}{x}\leq\frac{\varphi^{*}(4kx)-\varphi^{*}(4(k-1)x)}{4x},

which follows from the convexity of
\varphi^{*}
if
k\geq 2
.

(5) The statement follows from the following inequality
