\exists L\geq 1~\forall t\geq 0~\forall s\in\mathbb{N}:L^{s}\varphi^{*}(t)+sL^{s}t\leq\varphi^{*}(L^{s}t)+\sum_{i=1}^{s}L^{i},

if we choose
s
such that
e^{s}\geq\sigma
and set
t:=\rho k
,

H:=L^{s}
and
C:=\exp(\tfrac{1}{H\rho}\sum_{i=1}^{s}L^{i})
.
By
11.1
(1), there exists
L_{1}\geq 1
such that
\omega(2t)\leq L_{1}\omega(t)+L_{1}
for all
t\geq 0
and hence
there is
L\geq 1
such that
\varphi(t+1)\leq L\varphi(t)+L
for all
t\geq 0
.
Thus, for
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}(Lt)+L=\sup_{s\geq 0}(Lts-(\varphi(s)-L))\geq\sup_{s\geq 1}(Lts-L\varphi(s-1))=L\varphi^{*}(t)+Lt,

and the desired inequality follows by iteration.
∎

11.4.
Weight matrix description of Braun–Meise–Taylor classes

Theorem 11.4

(
[
74
]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a weight function with associated weight matrix
\mathfrak{W}=\{W^{x}\}_{x>0}
.
Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open and
K\subseteq U
compact.

(1)

For each
x>0
we have the continuous inclusions

\mathcal{E}^{\{W^{x}\}}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)

and
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{(W^{x})}(U)
.

(2)

As locally convex spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(K)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{proj}_{x>0}\mathcal{E}^{(W^{x})}(K)=\operatorname{proj}_{x>0}\operatorname{proj}_{\rho>0}\mathcal{E}^{W^{x}}_{\rho}(K),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(K)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{ind}_{x>0}\mathcal{E}^{\{W^{x}\}}(K)=\operatorname{ind}_{x>0}\operatorname{ind}_{\rho>0}\mathcal{E}^{W^{x}}_{\rho}(K),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{proj}_{K\subseteq_{cp}U}\operatorname{proj}_{x>0}\mathcal{E}^{(W^{x})}(K)=\operatorname{proj}_{x>0}\mathcal{E}^{(W^{x})}(U),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{proj}_{K\subseteq_{cp}U}\operatorname{ind}_{x>0}\mathcal{E}^{\{W^{x}\}}(K).

Proof.

(1) Let
x>0
be fixed.
If
f\in\mathcal{E}^{\{W^{x}\}}(U)
,
then for each compact subset
K\subseteq U
there exists
\rho>0
such that
\|f\|^{W^{x}}_{K,\rho}<\infty
.
By
Lemma

11.3
(5) and (
11.2
), there exist constants
H,C\geq 1
such that

\infty>C\|f\|^{W^{x}}_{K,\rho}\geq\|f\|^{W^{Hx}}_{K,1}=\|f\|^{\omega}_{K,Hx},

whence
f\in\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)
.

Assume that
f\in\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)
.
Let
x>0
and
\rho>0
be fixed. By
Lemma

11.3
(5),
there exist constants
H,C\geq 1
such that

W^{x}_{k}\leq C\rho^{k}W^{Hx}_{k}
for all
k
.
For each compact subset
K\subseteq U
we have

\|f\|^{\omega}_{K,\frac{x}{H}}<\infty
, and, thus, using (
11.2
),

\infty>C\|f\|^{\omega}_{K,\frac{x}{H}}=C\|f\|^{W^{\frac{x}{H}}}_{K,1}\geq\|f\|^{W^{x}}_{K,\rho}.

Since
\rho>0
was arbitrary, we may conclude that
f\in\mathcal{E}^{(W^{x})}(U)
.

(2) now follows from (1),
since we have the continuous inclusions
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(K)\supseteq\operatorname{proj}_{x>0}\mathcal{E}^{(W^{x})}(K)
and

\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(K)\subseteq\operatorname{ind}_{x>0}\mathcal{E}^{\{W^{x}\}}(K)
by definition.
∎

Corollary 11.5
.

Let
\omega
be a weight function. Then:

(1)

\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)
is a ring.

(2)

If
C^{\omega}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)
and
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{m}\supseteq V\to U
is real analytic,
then the pullback
\psi^{*}:\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)\to\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(V)
is well-defined.

(3)

\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)
is stable under differentiation.

Proof.

(1) and (2) follow from the fact that each
W^{x}
is a weight sequence; cf.
Section

2.5
.
(3) is a consequence of
Lemma

11.3
(3).
∎

11.5.
Braun–Meise–Taylor vs. Denjoy–Carleman classes

Theorem

11.4
shows that every Braun–Meise–Taylor class can be represented as a union or an intersection
of Denjoy–Carleman classes.
The question arises as to when Braun–Meise–Taylor and Denjoy–Carleman classes coincide.

Theorem 11.6
.

Let
\omega
be a weight function with associated weight matrix
\mathfrak{W}=\{W^{x}\}_{x>0}
.
Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open and non-empty. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\exists H\geq 1~\forall t\geq 0:2\omega(t)\leq\omega(Ht)+H
.

(2)

The weight sequences
W^{x}
and
W^{y}
are equivalent for all
x,y>0
.

(3)

\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)=\mathcal{E}^{\{W^{x}\}}(U)
for all
x>0
.

(4)

\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)=\mathcal{E}^{(W^{x})}(U)
for all
x>0
.

(5)

W^{x}
has moderate growth for some (equivalently, for all)
x>0
.

The equivalence of (1) and (2) can be seen by straightforward computations.
Then
(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)\Leftrightarrow(4)
follow from
Theorem

11.4
.
For the equivalence with (5) see
[
74
]
.
Conversely, one may ask when for a given weight sequence
M
there exists a weight function
\omega

such that
\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(U)=\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)
.

Theorem 11.7

(
[
16
, Theorem 14]
)
.

Let
M=(M_{k})
be a derivation-closed weight sequence with
m_{k}^{1/k}\to\infty
.
The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a weight function
\omega
such that for all open
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
we
have
\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)=\mathcal{E}^{[M]}(U)
as vector spaces and/or as locally convex spaces.

(2)

The weight sequence
M
has moderate growth and satisfies

\exists Q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}:\liminf_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mu_{Qk}}{\mu_{k}}>1.

(3)

The weight sequence
M
has moderate growth, the associated function
\omega_{M}(t)=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\log\frac{t^{k}}{M_{k}}
is a weight function, and
(1) holds with
\omega=\omega_{M}
.

Example 11.8
.

(1) Let
s\geq 1
. Then
\gamma_{s}(t):=\max\{0,t^{\frac{1}{s}}-1\}
is a weight function and we have the identity

\mathcal{E}^{[\gamma_{s}]}(U)=\mathcal{E}^{[G^{s}]}(U),

where
G^{s}
denotes the Gevrey sequence
G^{s}_{k}=k!^{s}
. See also
Example

12.3
.

(2) For
s>1
,
\omega_{s}(t):=\max\{0,(\log t)^{s}\}
is a weight function with associated weight matrix
\mathfrak{W}^{s}=\{W^{s,x}\}_{x>0}
given by

W^{s,x}_{k}=\exp(C_{s}\,x^{1/(s-1)}\,k^{s/(s-1)})
.
The classes
\mathcal{E}^{[\omega_{s}]}
are
not
Denjoy–Carleman classes.

11.6.
Weight matrices and associated ultradifferentiable classes

The weight matrix description of Braun–Meise–Taylor classes motivates the definition of ultradifferentiable classes by general weight matrices,
providing a general framework for ultradifferentiability.

Definition 11.9
.

A
weight matrix
is by definition a family
\mathfrak{M}
of weight sequences

M\in\mathfrak{M}
which is totally ordered with respect to the natural order relation on sequences (i.e., for all
M,N\in\mathfrak{M}
we have
M\leq N
or
N\leq M
).

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a weight matrix. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
open and
K\subseteq U
compact. We define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{M}\}}(K)

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{M\in\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(K),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}(K)

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{M\in\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(K),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{M}\}}(U)

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{K\subseteq_{cp}U}\bigcup_{M\in\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(K)=\bigcap_{K\subseteq_{cp}U}\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{M}\}}(K),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}(U)

\displaystyle:=\bigcap_{K\subseteq_{cp}U}\bigcap_{M\in\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(K)=\bigcap_{K\subseteq_{cp}U}\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}(K)=\bigcap_{M\in\mathfrak{M}}\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(U)

and endow these spaces with their natural locally convex topologies.
The class
\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}(U)
is said to be of
Beurling type

and the class
\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{M}\}}(U)
of
Roumieu type
.
Again we use
\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{M}]}(U)
as placeholder for either
\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}(U)

or
\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{M}\}}(U)
.

For each weight matrix
\mathfrak{M}
there exists a countable weight matrix
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{M}]}(U)=\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}]}(U)

as locally convex spaces; see
[
40
, Lemma 2.5]
.

Note that all
\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{M}]}(U)
are rings and if
C^{\omega}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{M}]}(U)

then the class
\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{M}]}
is stable by analytic change of coordinates.

11.7.
Inclusion relations

The inclusion relations for the classes
\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{M}]}
follow easily from those for Denjoy–Carleman classes.
For weight matrices
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}(\preccurlyeq)\mathfrak{N}\quad

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\quad\forall N\in\mathfrak{N}~\exists M\in\mathfrak{M}:M\preccurlyeq N,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}\{\preccurlyeq\}\mathfrak{N}\quad

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\quad\forall M\in\mathfrak{M}~\exists N\in\mathfrak{N}:M\preccurlyeq N,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}(\preccurlyeq\}\mathfrak{N}\quad

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\quad\exists M\in\mathfrak{M}~\exists N\in\mathfrak{N}:M\preccurlyeq N,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}\{\lhd)\mathfrak{N}\quad

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\quad\forall M\in\mathfrak{M}~\forall N\in\mathfrak{N}:M\lhd N.

Moreover, we set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}[\approx]\mathfrak{N}\quad

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathfrak{M}[\preccurlyeq]\mathfrak{N}[\preccurlyeq]\mathfrak{M}.

Lemma 11.10

(
[
74
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
be two weight matrices. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open and non-empty. Then:

(1)

\mathfrak{M}[\preccurlyeq]\mathfrak{N}
if and only if

\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{M}]}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{N}]}(U)
.

(2)

\mathfrak{M}(\preccurlyeq\}\mathfrak{N}
if and only if

\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{N}\}}(U)
.

(3)

\mathfrak{M}\{\lhd)\mathfrak{N}
if and only if

\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{M}\}}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{N})}(U)
.

All inclusions are continuous. For the “if” part it suffices to have the inclusions on
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.

Corollary 11.11
.

A class of Roumieu type and a class of Beurling type
can never coincide.

Proof.

Observe that

\mathfrak{M}(\preccurlyeq\}\mathfrak{N}\{\lhd)\mathfrak{M}
is impossible
for any two weight matrices
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
and apply
Lemma

11.10
.
∎

In the special case of Braun–Meise–Taylor classes we obtain

Proposition 11.12
.

Let
\omega
and
\sigma
be weight functions. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open and non-empty. Then:

(1)

\sigma(t)=O(\omega(t))
as
t\to\infty
if and only if

\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{[\sigma]}(U)
.

(2)

\sigma(t)=o(\omega(t))
as
t\to\infty
if and only if

\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{(\sigma)}(U)
.

(3)

\sigma(t)=O(\omega(t))
as
t\to\infty
if and only if

\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\{\sigma\}}(U)
.

All inclusions are continuous. For the “if” part it suffices to have the inclusions on
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.

Note that
\omega
and
\sigma
are said to be
equivalent
if

\sigma(t)=O(\omega(t))
and
\omega(t)=O(\sigma(t))
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{W}=\{W^{x}\}_{x>0}
and
\mathfrak{S}=\{S^{x}\}_{x>0}
be the associated weight matrices of

\omega
and
\sigma
, respectively.
Let us prove (1); (2) and (3) can be shown similarly.
In view of
Lemma

11.10
it suffices to prove

(11.3)

\sigma(t)=O(\omega(t))
as
t\to\infty
if and only if
\mathfrak{W}[\preccurlyeq]\mathfrak{S}
.

Assume that
\sigma(t)=O(\omega(t))
as
t\to\infty
. Then there exists
H\geq 1
such that
\sigma(t)\leq H\omega(t)+H

for all
t\geq 0
. This implies
H\varphi_{\omega}^{*}(t)\leq\varphi_{\sigma}^{*}(Ht)+H
for the Young conjugates of
t\mapsto\sigma(e^{t})
and
t\mapsto\omega(e^{t})
. In particular, for
t=xk
we find

W^{x}_{k}=\exp(\tfrac{1}{x}\varphi_{\omega}^{*}(xk))\leq\exp(\tfrac{1}{Hx}\varphi_{\sigma}^{*}(Hxk)+\tfrac{1}{x})=e^{1/x}S^{Hx}_{k}.

That means there is
H\geq 1
such that for all
x>0
we have
W^{x}\leq e^{1/x}S^{Hx}

which implies
\mathfrak{W}(\preccurlyeq)\mathfrak{S}
as well as
\mathfrak{W}\{\preccurlyeq\}\mathfrak{S}
.

For the converse direction we assume
\mathfrak{W}\{\preccurlyeq\}\mathfrak{S}
; for the assumption
\mathfrak{W}(\preccurlyeq)\mathfrak{S}

the arguments are analogous.
This means, using
Lemma

11.3
(5),

\forall x>0~\exists y>0~\exists C>0~\forall k\in\mathbb{N}:\tfrac{1}{x}\varphi_{\omega}^{*}(xk)\leq\tfrac{1}{y}\varphi_{\sigma}^{*}(yk)+C.

By the convexity of
\varphi_{\omega}^{*}
and
\varphi_{\sigma}^{*}
we may conclude that

\forall x>0~\exists y>0~\exists D>0~\forall t\geq 0:\tfrac{1}{x}\varphi_{\omega}^{*}(xt)\leq\tfrac{1}{y}\varphi_{\sigma}^{*}(yt)+C

(for different
y
and
C
). Then

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{y}\varphi_{\sigma}(t)=\sup_{s\geq 0}(ts-\tfrac{1}{y}\varphi_{\sigma}^{*}(ys))\leq\sup_{s\geq 0}(ts-\tfrac{1}{x}\varphi_{\omega}^{*}(xs))+C=\tfrac{1}{x}\varphi_{\omega}(t)+C,

that is

\tfrac{1}{y}\sigma(t)\leq\tfrac{1}{x}\omega(t)+C

which implies
\sigma(t)=O(\omega(t))
as
t\to\infty
.
∎

Corollary 11.13
.

Let
\omega
be a weight function. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open and non-empty.

(1)

\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{C}^{n})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)
if and only if

\omega(t)=O(t)
as
t\to\infty
.

(2)

C^{\omega}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)
if and only if

\omega(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
.

(3)

C^{\omega}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(U)
if and only if

\omega(t)=O(t)
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

This follows from
Example

11.8
(1) and
Proposition

11.12
.
∎

11.8.
Intersection and union of all non-quasianalytic Gevrey classes

Let
\mathfrak{G}:=\{G^{s}\}_{s>1}
be the weight matrix consisting of all non-quasianalytic Gevrey sequences
G^{s}_{k}=k!^{s}
.
It turns out that
\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{G})}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{G}\}}
are neither Denjoy–Carleman nor Braun–Meise–Taylor classes.

Theorem 11.14

(
[
74
]
)
.

The weight matrix
\mathfrak{G}=\{G^{s}\}_{s>1}
has the following properties:

(1)

We have
L\lhd G^{s}\lhd G^{t}
for all
1<s<t
, where
L=(L_{k})
is the weight sequence
L_{k}:=k^{k}(\log(k+e))^{2k}
.

(2)

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open and
K\subseteq U
compact. As locally convex spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{G})}(K)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{proj}_{s>1}\mathcal{E}^{(G^{s})}(K)=\operatorname{proj}_{s>1}\mathcal{E}^{\{G^{s}\}}(K),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{G}\}}(K)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{ind}_{s>1}\mathcal{E}^{\{G^{s}\}}(K)=\operatorname{ind}_{s>1}\mathcal{E}^{(G^{s})}(K),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{G})}(U)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{proj}_{s>1}\mathcal{E}^{(G^{s})}(U)=\operatorname{proj}_{s>1}\mathcal{E}^{\{G^{s}\}}(U),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{G}\}}(U)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{proj}_{K\subseteq_{cp}U}\operatorname{ind}_{s>1}\mathcal{E}^{\{G^{s}\}}(K)=\operatorname{proj}_{K\subseteq_{cp}U}\operatorname{ind}_{s>1}\mathcal{E}^{(G^{s})}(K).

(3)

\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{G})}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{G}\}}
are non-quasianalytic.

(4)

Neither
\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{G})}
nor

\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{G}\}}
coincides with a Denjoy–Carleman or a Braun–Meise–Taylor class (as sets).

Proof.

(1) If
1<s<t
, then

\Big(\frac{G^{s}_{k}}{G^{t}_{k}}\Big)^{1/k}=k!^{\frac{s-t}{k}}\leq k^{s-t}\to 0

and

\Big(\frac{L_{k}}{G^{s}_{k}}\Big)^{1/k}=\frac{k}{k!^{s/k}}(\log(k+e))^{2}\leq ek^{1-s}(\log(k+e))^{2}\to 0.

(2) This follows from the definition and from the fact that
G^{s}\lhd G^{t}
if

s<t
.

(3) is a consequence of (1), since the weight sequence
L
is non-quasianalytic. It also follows from
Theorem

11.16
.

(4)
Suppose that there is a weight sequence
M
such that

\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{G})}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then
Lemma

11.10
implies
M(\preccurlyeq)\mathfrak{G}(\preccurlyeq)M
,
i.e.,
M\preccurlyeq G^{s}

for all
s>1
and there exists
t>1
such that
G^{t}\preccurlyeq M
.
Thus
M
is equivalent to
G^{t}
and hence

\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{(G^{t})}(\mathbb{R})\supsetneq\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{G})}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{(M)}(\mathbb{R}),

a contradiction.
Similarly,
if
\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{G}\}}(\mathbb{R})
for
some weight sequence
M
, then
we conclude
M\{\preccurlyeq\}\mathfrak{G}\{\preccurlyeq\}M
,
i.e.,
M\preccurlyeq G^{s}

for some
s>1
and
G^{t}\preccurlyeq M
for all
t>1
.
So
M
is equivalent to
G^{s}
which leads to the contradiction

\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{\{G^{s}\}}(\mathbb{R})\subsetneq\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{G}\}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}(\mathbb{R}).

Now assume that there exists a weight function
\omega
such that

\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{G})}(\mathbb{R})
.
Let
\mathfrak{W}=\{W^{x}\}_{x>0}
be the weight matrix associated with
\omega
.
Then
\mathfrak{W}(\preccurlyeq)\mathfrak{G}(\preccurlyeq)\mathfrak{W}
,
i.e., for all
t>1
there exists
x>0
such that
W^{x}\preccurlyeq G^{t}
and
for all
y>0
there exists
s>1
such that
G^{s}\preccurlyeq W^{y}
.
It follows that

\forall y>0~\exists s>1~\exists x>0:W^{x}\preccurlyeq G^{s}\preccurlyeq W^{y}.

As a consequence
\mathcal{E}^{[W^{x}]}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{[G^{s}]}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{[W^{y}]}(\mathbb{R})
.
But, by
Example

11.8
(1), we have
\mathcal{E}^{[G^{s}]}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{[\gamma_{s}]}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\gamma_{s}(t)=t^{1/s}
.
Consequently,

\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{(W^{x})}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{(G^{s})}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{(\gamma_{s})}(\mathbb{R})

and thus
\gamma_{s}(t)=O(\omega(t))
as
t\to\infty
, by
Proposition

11.12
.
On the other hand,

\mathcal{E}^{\{\gamma_{s}\}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{\{G^{s}\}}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\{W^{y}\}}(\mathbb{R})\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(\mathbb{R})

and hence
\omega(t)=O(\gamma_{s}(t))
as
t\to\infty
.
But this leads to a contradiction:

\mathcal{E}^{(G^{s})}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{(\gamma_{s})}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{G})}(\mathbb{R})\subsetneq\mathcal{E}^{(G^{s})}(\mathbb{R}).

In analogy, one shows that
\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(\mathbb{R})=\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{G}\}}(\mathbb{R})
for some weight function
\omega

is impossible.

In view of
Corollary

11.11
the proof of (4) is complete.
∎

Remark 11.15
.

The classes
\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{G}]}
enjoy good properties that the single Gevrey classes do not have,
as investigated in
[
28
]
.
In fact, if
M
is a strongly log-convex weight sequence of moderate growth such that the “power”

M^{(a)}:=(k!\,m_{k}^{a})
is non-quasianalytic for every
a>0
(e.g. strongly regular sequences or

M_{k}:=k!\,\exp(\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\log j)^{\delta})
for
\delta\in(0,1]
),
then the class
\bigcap_{a>0}\mathcal{B}^{\{M^{(a)}\}}
admits versions of

(1)

Whitney’s extension theorem,

(2)

Łojasiewicz’s theorem on regularly situated compact sets,

(3)

Weierstrass’ division and preparation theorems,

(4)

Whitney’s spectral theorem.

Let us give a short argument for (1):
for any jet
F
of class
\bigcap_{a>0}\mathcal{B}^{\{M^{(a)}\}}
on a compact set
K

one can find a strongly log-convex non-quasianalytic weight sequence
L
of moderate growth
such that
L\lhd M^{(a)}
for all
a>0
and
F
is of class
\mathcal{E}^{\{L\}}
; see
[
28
, Proposition 5]
.
Then, if
L_{k}=k!\,\ell_{k}
and
\lambda_{k}^{*}:=\frac{\ell_{k}}{\ell_{k-1}}
, we find

\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{1}{j(\lambda_{j}^{*})^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}^{*}}\sum_{j\geq k}\frac{1}{j\lambda_{j}^{*}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\lambda_{k}^{*}},

and hence
F
has an extension
f
in
\mathcal{B}^{\{L^{(2)}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, by
Theorem

10.4
.
But

\Big(\frac{L_{k}^{(2)}}{M_{k}^{(a)}}\Big)^{1/k}=\Big(\frac{\ell_{k}^{2}}{m_{k}^{a}}\Big)^{1/k}=\Big(\frac{\ell_{k}}{m_{k}^{a/2}}\Big)^{2/k}=\Big(\frac{L_{k}}{M_{k}^{(a/2)}}\Big)^{2/k}\to 0,

for all
a>0
so that
f\in\bigcap_{a>0}\mathcal{B}^{\{M^{(a)}\}}
.

11.9.
Quasianalyticity and non-quasianalyticity

The weight matrix description of Braun–Meise–Taylor classes, i.e.
Theorem

11.4
,
allows us to deduce the characterization of (non-)quasianalyticity from the Denjoy–Carleman theorem
3.6
.
For a different proof relying on Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-method and a Paley–Wiener theorem see
[
19
]
.

Theorem 11.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a weight matrix. Then:

(1)

\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{M}\}}
is quasianalytic if and only if all
M\in\mathfrak{M}
are quasianalytic.

(2)

\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}
is quasianalytic if and only if some
M\in\mathfrak{M}
is quasianalytic.

Proof.

(1) If some
M\in\mathfrak{M}
is non-quasianalytic, then there exists a
non-trivial function with compact support of class
\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}
, thus of of class
\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{M}\}}
.
Conversely, any
\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{M}\}}
-function with compact support is a

\mathcal{E}^{\{M\}}
-function for some
M\in\mathfrak{M}
.

(2) If some
M\in\mathfrak{M}
is quasianalytic, then clearly
\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}
is quasianalytic since

\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{(M)}
.
Conversely, assume that all
M\in\mathfrak{M}
are non-quasianalytic. We may assume that the weight matrix

\mathfrak{M}
is countable (
[
40
, Lemma 2.5]
),
i.e.,
\mathfrak{M}=\{M^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
M^{n}\geq M^{n+1}
for all
n
.
Set
\alpha^{n}_{k}:=(M^{n}_{k})^{-1/k}
. Then, for each
n
the sequence
\alpha^{n}
is decreasing and
\sum_{k}a^{n}_{k}<\infty
, by
Remark

3.9
.
Moreover
\alpha^{n}\leq\alpha^{n+1}
.

We claim that there is a decreasing positive sequence
\alpha
such that
\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}<\infty

and for each
n
there exists
k_{n}
such that
\alpha_{k}\geq\alpha^{n}_{k}
for all
k\geq k_{n}
.
Then we may use
Corollary

3.5
and
Remark

3.9
as in the proof of
Theorem

3.6
to see that

\mathcal{E}^{(M)}
with
M_{k}:=a_{k}^{-k}
, thus also
\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{M})}
, is non-quasianalytic.

It remains to show the claim; the proof is based on ideas from
[
82
, Proposition 4.7]
.
We define recursively two increasing sequences of integers
(p_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(q_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Set
p_{0}=q_{0}=0
and let

•

p_{j}
be the minimal integer such that
p_{j}>q_{j-1}
and
\sum_{k>p_{j}}\alpha^{j+1}_{k}\leq 2^{-j}
,

•

q_{j}
be the minimal integer such that
\alpha^{j}_{p_{j}}>\alpha^{j+1}_{q_{j}+1}
.

This makes sense since
\alpha^{n}_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
for each
n
.
Moreover it implies
q_{j}\geq p_{j}
.
Then we define

\alpha_{k}:=\begin{cases}\alpha^{j}_{k}&\text{ if }q_{j-1}<k\leq p_{j},\\
\alpha^{j}_{p_{j}}&\text{ if }p_{j}<k\leq q_{j}.\end{cases}

By construction
\alpha
is decreasing.
By the minimality of
q_{j}
we have
\alpha^{j}_{p_{j}}\leq\alpha^{j+1}_{k}
for
p_{j}<k\leq q_{j}
. Thus

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\alpha_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\Big(\sum_{p_{j}<k\leq q_{j}}\alpha^{j}_{p_{j}}+\sum_{q_{j}<k\leq p_{j+1}}\alpha^{j+1}_{k}\Big)\leq\sum_{j}\Big(\sum_{p_{j}<k\leq p_{j+1}}\alpha^{j+1}_{k}\Big)\leq\sum_{j}2^{-j}.

For fixed
n
we have
a_{k}\geq a^{n}_{k}
for all
k\geq q_{n-1}
. The claim is proved.
∎

Theorem 11.17
.

Let
\omega
be a weight function with associated weight matrix
\mathfrak{W}=\{W^{x}\}_{x>0}
.
The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}
is quasianalytic.

(2)

\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}
is quasianalytic.

(3)

The weight function
\omega
is
quasianalytic
, i.e.,

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(t)}{t^{2}}\,dt=\infty.

(4)

The weight sequence
W^{x}
is quasianalytic for all
x>0
.

(5)

The weight sequence
W^{x}
is quasianalytic for some
x>0
.

Proof.

It suffices to show the equivalence of (3), (4), and (5). The rest follows from
Theorem

11.16
.
Now
W^{x}
is quasianalytic if and only if the associated function
\omega_{W^{x}}(t)=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\log\frac{t^{k}}{W^{x}_{k}}
satisfies

\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega_{W^{x}}(t)}{t^{2}}\,dt=\infty
; cf.
[
47
, Lemma 4.1]
. Each
\omega_{W^{x}}
is equivalent to
\omega
; cf.
[
74
, Lemma 5.7]
.
∎

Note that a
non-quasianalytic
weight function
\omega
necessarily
satisfies
\omega(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
,
i.e.,

C^{\omega}(U)\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)
.
Indeed, since
\omega
is increasing,

\displaystyle\frac{\omega(t)}{t}=\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(t)}{s^{2}}\,ds\leq\int_{t}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(s)}{s^{2}}\,ds\to 0\quad\text{ as }t\to\infty.

11.10.
Stability properties

We state without proof a characterization of stability under composition for Braun–Meise–Taylor classes;
for the general classes
\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{M}]}
we refer to
[
74
]
.

Theorem 11.18

(
[
36
]
,
[
74
]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a weight function satisfying
\omega(t)=O(t)
as
t\to\infty
.
The following conditions are equivalent.

(1)

\exists C>0~\exists t_{0}>0~\forall\lambda\geq 1~\forall t\geq t_{0}:\omega(\lambda t)\leq C\lambda\omega(t)
.

(2)

\omega
is equivalent to a concave weight function.

(3)

\omega
is equivalent to a subadditive weight function.

(4)

\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}
is stable under composition.

(5)

\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}
is stable under composition.

The condition
\omega(t)=O(t)
as
t\to\infty
is only used in the directions
(4)
\Rightarrow
(1)
and (5)
\Rightarrow
(1).

That subadditivity is a sufficient condition for stability under composition is seen as follows:
Let
\mathfrak{W}=\{W^{x}\}_{x>0}
be the associated weight matrix.
We have

\displaystyle W^{x}_{k}

\displaystyle=\exp(\tfrac{1}{x}\varphi^{*}(xk))=\exp\sup_{t\geq 1}(k\log(t)-\tfrac{1}{x}\omega(t))=\sup_{t\geq 1}\big(t^{k}e^{-\tfrac{1}{x}\omega(t)}\big).

Thus subadditivity of
\omega
implies

\displaystyle w^{x}_{j}w^{x}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sup_{t,s\geq 1}\Big(\frac{t^{j}s^{k}}{j!k!}e^{-\tfrac{1}{x}(\omega(t)+\omega(s))}\Big)\leq\sup_{t,s\geq 1}\Big(\frac{(t+s)^{j+k}}{(j+k)!}e^{-\tfrac{1}{x}\omega(t+s)}\Big)=w^{x}_{j+k}.

Together with
Lemma

11.3
(3) we see that,
for all
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{>0}
with
\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{j}=k
,

\displaystyle w^{x}_{j}w^{x}_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots w^{x}_{\alpha_{j}}\leq w_{1}^{j}w^{2x}_{j}w^{2x}_{\alpha_{1}-1}\cdots w^{2x}_{\alpha_{j}-1}\leq w_{1}^{j}w^{2x}_{k}

which implies
(W^{x})^{\circ}\preccurlyeq W^{2x}
for all
x>0
. From this stability under composition follows easily (cf.
Section

2.5
).

Remark 11.19
.

Let
\omega
be a weight function with
\omega(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
.
That
\omega
is equivalent to a concave weight function is furthermore equivalent to any of the following conditions:

•

There is a weight matrix
\mathfrak{S}
consisting of strongly log-convex weight sequences such that
\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}=\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{S}\}}
.

•

There is a weight matrix
\mathfrak{S}
consisting of strongly log-convex weight sequences such that
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}=\mathcal{E}^{(\mathfrak{S})}
.

•

\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}
can be described by almost analytic extensions.

•

\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}
can be described by almost analytic extensions.

For all this (including the meaning of
almost analytic extensions
) we refer to
[
40
, Theorem 4.8]
and

[
79
, Theorem 11]
.
Furthermore, these conditions are equivalent to the classes
\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}
to be stable under inverse/implicit functions and solving ODEs (in the sense described in
Section

2.5
),
respectively;
see
[
75
]
.

12.
Extension in Braun–Meise–Taylor classes

12.1.
Whitney ultrajets

Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a closed non-empty set. Let
\omega
be a weight function.
A Whitney jet
F=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\in\mathcal{E}(A)

is called a
\omega
-Whitney ultrajet of Beurling type
on
A

if for all compact subsets
K\subseteq A
and all integers
m\geq 1

we have

(12.1)

\|F\|^{\omega}_{K,1/m}:=\sup_{x\in K}\sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}|F^{\alpha}(x)|\exp\big(-m\varphi^{*}\big(\tfrac{|\alpha|}{m}\big)\big)<\infty

and

(12.2)

|F|^{\omega}_{K,1/m}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in K\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}\sup_{p\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{|\alpha|\leq p}|(R^{p}_{x}F)^{\alpha}(y)|\frac{(p+1-|\alpha|)!}{|x-y|^{p+1-|\alpha|}}\exp\big(-m\varphi^{*}\big(\tfrac{p+1}{m}\big)\big)<\infty.

We denote by
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(A)

the locally convex space of all
\omega
-Whitney ultrajets
F
of Beurling type on
A
equipped with the projective limit topology
with respect to the system of seminorms
\|F\|^{\omega}_{K,1/m}+|F|^{\omega}_{K,1/m}
.
The space of
\omega
-Whitney ultrajets of Roumieu type
on
A

is

\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(A):=\{F\in\mathcal{E}(A):\forall K\subseteq_{cp}A~\exists m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}:\|F\|^{\omega}_{K,m}+|F|^{\omega}_{K,m}<\infty\}

supplied with its natural locally convex topology.
In view of
Lemma

11.3
(5), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(A)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{proj}_{K\subseteq_{cp}A}\operatorname{proj}_{m>0}\mathcal{E}^{(W^{1/m})}(K),

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(A)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{proj}_{K\subseteq_{cp}A}\operatorname{ind}_{m>0}\mathcal{E}^{\{W^{m}\}}(K),

where
\mathfrak{W}=\{W^{x}\}_{x>0}
is the associated weight matrix of
\omega
.

12.2.
Strong weight functions

We shall see that
\mathcal{E}^{[\omega]}
admits extension theorems preserving the class if and only if
\omega
is a
strong
weight function.

Definition 12.1
.

A non-quasianalytic weight function
\omega
is called
strong
if

(12.3)

\;\exists C>0\;\forall t>0:\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(ut)}{u^{2}}\,du\leq C\omega(t)+C.

Lemma 12.2

(
[
61
, Propositions 1.3 and 1.7]
)
.

Let
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be an increasing function with
\omega(0)=0
and
\omega(t)\to\infty
as
t\to\infty
.
The following conditions are equivalent:
14
14

14

If
\omega
satisfies these equivalent conditions, then
\omega(t)=O(t^{\alpha})
as
t\to\infty
for some
0<\alpha<1
;
see
[
61
, Corollary 1.4]
. That means the class contains a Gevrey class (of Roumieu or Beurling type, respectively).

(1)

\omega
satisfies (
12.3
).

(2)

There exist constants
K>H>1
such that
\omega(Kt)\leq H\omega(t)
for all sufficiently large
t
.

(3)

The increasing concave function

(12.4)

\kappa(t):=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(ut)}{u^{2}}\,du,\quad t>0,

satisfies (
12.3
) and
\omega\leq\kappa\leq C\omega+C
for some
C>0
.

(4)

The harmonic extension

(12.5)

P_{\omega}(x+iy):=\begin{cases}\frac{|y|}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\omega(t)}{(t-x)^{2}+y^{2}}\,dt&\text{ if }|y|>0,\\
\omega(x)&\text{ if }y=0,\end{cases}

satisfies
P_{\omega}(z)=O(\omega(z))
as
|z|\to\infty
, where
\omega(z):=\omega(|z|)
.

Example 12.3
.

(1)
\omega_{s}(t)=\max(0,(\log t)^{s})
is a strong weight function for each
s>1
.

(2) Let
M=(M_{k})
be a strongly regular weight sequence.
Then
\omega_{M}
satisfies (
12.3
), by
[
47
, Proposition 4.4]
.
By
Lemma

12.2
(3), we have
\omega_{M}\leq\kappa\leq C\omega_{M}+C
. Since
\kappa
is subadditive,

\omega_{M}(2t)\leq\kappa(2t)\leq 2\kappa(t)\leq 2C\omega_{M}(t)+2C,

i.e.,
\omega_{M}
satisfies
11.1
(1) and hence is a weight function (the other conditions are always
fulfilled by
\omega_{M}
).
Moreover,
2\omega_{M}(t)\leq\omega_{M}(Dt)+D
, as
M
has moderate growth, see
[
47
, Proposition 3.6]
.
Then
Theorem

11.6
applied to
\omega_{M}
shows that
\mathcal{E}^{[M]}=\mathcal{E}^{[\omega_{M}]}
,
since
M_{k}=\sup_{t\geq 0}\frac{t^{k}}{\exp(\omega_{M}(t))}=e^{\varphi^{*}(k)}=W^{1}_{k}
(cf.
[
47
, Proposition 3.2]
). Consequently, the extension results for strong weight functions
comprise those for strongly regular weight sequences.

12.3.
The singleton and other sets with nice geometry

It was shown in
[
61
]
that Whitney ultrajets of class
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}
admit extension from sets with nice geometry, including the singleton
\{0\}
.

Theorem 12.4

(
[
61
, Theorem 3.10]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a non-quasianalytic weight function.
The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\omega
is strong.

(2)

j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\{0\})
is surjective.

(3)

j^{\infty}_{K}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(K)
is surjective for all compact convex
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with non-empty interior.

(4)

j^{\infty}_{\overline{\Omega}}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\overline{\Omega})
is surjective for all bounded open
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with real analytic boundary.

Let us sketch an argument for the equivalence of (1) and (2).
The Fourier–Laplace transform is a linear topological isomorphism between
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})_{b}^{\prime}
and the weighted space of entire functions

\mathcal{A}_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{n}):=\Big\{f\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{C}^{n}):\;\exists j\in\mathbb{N}:\sup_{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}}|f(z)|e^{-j(|\mathrm{Im}z|+\omega(z))}<\infty\Big\}

equipped with its natural inductive limit topology; cf.
[
19
]
.
The dual
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\{0\})_{b}^{\prime}
can be identified with
(\Lambda^{(\omega)}_{n})_{b}^{\prime}
, where

\Lambda^{(\omega)}_{n}:=\Big\{(c_{\alpha})\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}^{n}}:\;\forall m\in\mathbb{N}:\sum_{\alpha}|c_{\alpha}|e^{-m\sum_{j=1}^{n}\varphi^{*}(\frac{\alpha_{j}}{m})}<\infty\Big\}.

Then the map
(\Lambda^{(\omega)}_{n})_{b}^{\prime}\ni(c_{\alpha})\mapsto(z\mapsto\sum_{\alpha}c_{\alpha}(-iz)^{\alpha})

is a linear topological isomorphism between
(\Lambda^{(\omega)}_{n})_{b}^{\prime}
and

\mathcal{A}_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n}):=\Big\{f\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{C}^{n}):\;\exists j\in\mathbb{N}:\sup_{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}}|f(z)|e^{-j\omega(z)}<\infty\Big\}

and we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{(\Lambda^{(\omega)}_{n})_{b}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(j^{\infty}_{\{0\}})^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\{0\})_{b}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{n})}

where the bottom arrow is the inclusion map.
So (2) holds if and only if the inclusion
\mathcal{A}_{2}(\mathbb{C}^{n})\to\mathcal{A}_{1}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
is an injective topological homomorphism.
It is shown in
[
61
]
that the latter holds if and only if
P_{\omega}(z)=O(\omega(z))
as
|z|\to\infty
;
the proof relies on the Phragmén–Lindelöf principle and Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates for the solution of the
\overline{\partial}
-problem.

Remark 12.5
.

An analogous result holds in the Roumieu case
\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}
, see
[
17
]
.

Concerning the existence of extension operators in the above cases we have

Theorem 12.6

(
[
63
]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a strong weight function.
Then

(1)

j^{\infty}_{\overline{\Omega}}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\overline{\Omega})
is split surjective for all bounded open
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with real analytic boundary.

(2)

j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\{0\})
is split surjective if and only if

(12.6)

\;\forall C>1\;\exists\delta>0\;\exists t_{0}>0\;\forall t\geq t_{0}:\omega^{-1}(Ct)\omega^{-1}(\delta t)\leq(\omega^{-1}(t))^{2}.

A weight function
\omega
satisfying (
12.6
) is called a
(DN)-weight
.

The proof of (1) is based on the splitting theorem of Vogt and Wagner
[
91
]
for the short exact sequence
of nuclear Fréchet spaces

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(\overline{B}\setminus\Omega)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(\overline{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\infty}_{\overline{\Omega}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\overline{\Omega})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where
B
is a large open ball containing
\overline{\Omega}
. By assumption,
K:=\overline{B}\setminus\Omega
is
the closure of a bounded open set with real analytic boundary
and, for such
K
, the space
\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(K)
has property (
\Omega
), see
[
63
, Corollary 2.9]
.
On the other hand
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\overline{\Omega})
has property (DN), by
[
62
, Proposition 5.7]
.

For the singleton, we note that
\omega
satisfies (
12.6
) if and only if
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\{0\})
has property (DN),
by
[
60
, Theorem 2.17 and Proposition 3.1]
. Thus, in dimension
n=1
the splitting theorem
can be applied as above. The case
n>1
follows by a tensor product argument; cf.
[
63
, 3.1]
.
Since
\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(\overline{B})
has (DN), by
[
62
, Lemma 1.10(b)]
, so does
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\{0\})
if
the sequence splits, since (DN) is inherited by closed subspaces.
See also
[
61
, Corollary 3.12]
.

Example 12.7
.

(1) If
M
is a strongly regular weight sequence, then
\omega_{M}
is a (DN)-weight.

(2) The weight functions
\omega_{s}(t)=\max\{0,(\log t)^{s}\}
,
s>1
, are strong, but not (DN)-weights.

Remark 12.8
.

The function
\omega(t)=\max\{0,\log t\}
is not a weight function, since condition
11.1
(2) is violated.
Nevertheless it satisfies (
12.3
) and violates (
12.6
). Then each seminorm
\|\cdot\|^{\omega}_{K,\rho}

vanishes identically.
So, formally, one may identify
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(U)
with
C^{\infty}(U)
.
Indeed,
the theory of extension operators in the Braun–Meise–Taylor setting of Beurling type on the one hand and in
the
C^{\infty}
setting on the other hand
have many similarities.

Remark 12.9
.

As for Denjoy–Carleman classes
the Borel map is never onto in the quasianalytic case:
Let
\omega
be a quasianalytic weight function such that
\omega(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
, i.e.,
the real analytic class is strictly contained in
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}
.
Then there exist elements in
\Lambda^{(\omega)}
that are not contained in the Borel image

j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}\mathcal{E}^{\{\sigma\}}_{0}
of
any
quasianalytic weight function
\sigma
.
This can be deduced from
Theorem

5.4
using the description of Braun–Meise–Taylor classes
given in
Theorem

11.4
. For details see
[
77
]
.

12.4.
Optimal cutoff functions

As in the Denjoy–Carleman setting, to address
the extension problem for general closed sets one needs cutoff functions with certain
optimal
estimates.
Such functions are obtained by constructing certain entire functions and applying the Paley–Wiener theorem.
This in turn is based on Hörmanders estimates for the solution of the
\overline{\partial}
-problem and ultimately
boils down to finding subharmonic functions on
\mathbb{C}
with suitable upper and lower bounds.

The optimal bounds are here expressed in terms of the conjugate
\omega^{\star}
:
for a function
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with
\omega(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
one defines

(12.7)

\omega^{\star}(t):=\sup_{s\geq 0}\big(\omega(s)-st\big),\quad t>0.

Then
\omega^{\star}
is decreasing, continuous, and convex
with
\omega^{\star}(t)\to\infty
as
t\to 0
, see
[
69
, Remark 1.5]
.
Since
\omega(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
,
\omega^{\star}(t)
is finite for all
t
.
If
\omega
is concave and increasing, then, by
[
69
, Proposition 1.6]
,

(12.8)

\omega(t)=\inf_{s>0}\big(\omega^{\star}(s)+st\big),\quad t>0.

Theorem 12.10

(
[
15
, Proposition 2.2]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a strong concave weight function.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
there exist
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,
C>0
and
0<r_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
such that
for all
0<r<r_{0}
there exist
\varphi_{n,r}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with the following properties:

(1)

0\leq\varphi_{n,r}\leq 1
.

(2)

\varphi_{n,r}|_{[-r,r]}=1
and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{n,r}\subseteq[-\frac{9}{8}r,\frac{9}{8}r]
.

(3)

We have

\|\varphi_{n,r}\|^{\omega}_{\mathbb{R},m}\leq C\exp\Big(\frac{1}{n}\omega^{\star}(nr)\Big).

The functions
\varphi_{n,r}
are useful for the Roumieu case. The following cutoff functions
are needed in the Beurling case.

Theorem 12.11

(
[
37
, Corollary 2.6]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a strong concave weight function.
There exist functions
(\varphi_{r})_{r>0}
in
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R})
such that:

(1)

0\leq\varphi_{r}\leq 1
.

(2)

\varphi_{r}|_{[-r,r]}=1
and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{r}\subseteq[-\frac{9}{8}r,\frac{9}{8}r]
.

(3)

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
there exist
C>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
such that for all
r>0

\|\varphi_{r}\|^{\omega}_{\mathbb{R},\frac{1}{m}}\leq C\exp(n\omega^{\star}(r)).

In combination with
Proposition

7.7
we easily obtain corresponding partitions of unity
subordinate to families of Whitney cubes for a given closed set
A
.

Remark 12.12
.

The existence of such
optimal
cutoff functions is equivalent to the fact that
\omega
is a strong
weight function and additionally to the exactness of certain
\overline{\partial}
-complexes, see
[
53
]
.
Recall that a strong weight function is equivalent to a strong concave weight function, by
Lemma

12.2
.

12.5.
Extension of Whitney ultrajets

Theorem 12.13

(
[
15
]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a non-quasianalytic weight function.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\omega
is strong.

(2)

j^{\infty}_{A}:\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(A)
is surjective for every closed subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(3)

j^{\infty}_{A}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(A)
is surjective for every closed subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The sufficiency of strongness of
\omega
for (2) is proved in analogy to
Theorem

8.4
:
Without loss of generality one may assume that
A
is compact. The
case of the singleton yields local extensions
f_{x}
,
x\in A
, which form a bounded set in
\mathcal{B}^{\{\omega\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, cf.
Lemma

8.2
.
The existence of an optimal partition of unity for a
family of Whitney cubes for
A
allows to define the required extension by a formula similar to (
8.9
).

The Beurling case (3) can be reduced to the Roumieu case (2) by a reduction lemma (see
[
15
]
) which is similar in
spirit to
Lemma

8.5
.

The necessity of (1) for (2) as well as for (3) follows from the special case of the singleton.
But is was shown in
[
1
]
that
\omega
must be strong, if there is
any
non-empty compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}

such that
j^{\infty}_{K}:\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(K)

or
j^{\infty}_{K}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(K)
is surjective.

12.6.
Extension operators

We saw in
Theorem

12.6
that
compact sets with real analytic boundary admit extension operators
in the Beurling case
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}
for all strong weight functions
\omega
.
The singleton
\{0\}
, on the other hand, admits an extension operator if and only if
\omega
is a strong (DN)-weight.
The existence of optimal cutoff functions of Beurling type (
Theorem

12.11
)
make it possible to extend this result to all closed sets.

Theorem 12.14

(
[
37
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a strong weight function.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\omega
is a (DN)-weight.

(2)

j^{\infty}_{A}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(A)
is split surjective for every closed subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The proof is analogous to the one of
Theorem

9.3
. Indeed, the case of the singleton guarantees that local extension operators

E_{x}
,
x\in A
, exist and
\{E_{x}:x\in A\}
is locally equicontinuous in
L(\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(A),\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.
And as in
Proposition

9.2
one shows that admitting an extension operator is a local property.

If
\omega
is strong but not a (DN)-weight, then
the existence of an extension operator depends on the set.
In fact, if
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a non-empty compact set
and
L
a compact neighborhood of
K
, then

(12.9)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(L,K)~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{\infty}_{K}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is an exact sequence of Fréchet spaces, by
Theorem

12.13
,
where

\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(L,K):=\{f\in\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):\operatorname{supp}f\subseteq L,\,j^{\infty}_{K}f=0\}

and
\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(L)

carry the subspace topology of
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
It is shown in
[
37
, Proposition 4.4]
that

\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(L,K)
has the property (
\Omega
); the proof is based on the result of Meise and Taylor
[
63
, Corollary 2.9]
that
\mathcal{D}^{(\omega)}(K)
has property (
\Omega
) if
K

has real analytic boundary and on the existence of optimal cutoff functions (
Theorem

12.11
).
Thus the splitting theorem of Vogt and Wagner
[
91
]
gives

Theorem 12.15

(
[
37
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a strong weight function and let
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a non-empty compact set.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(K)
has property (DN).

(2)

j^{\infty}_{K}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(K)
is split surjective.

Compact sets that do not admit extension operators are the

flat cusps

K_{f}=\{(x,y)\in[0,1]^{2}:|y|\leq|f(x)|\},

where
f\in\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R})
with
j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}f=0
.
If
\omega
is not a (DN)-weight, then
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(K_{f})
does not have (DN), and

j^{\infty}_{K}:\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(K)
is not split surjective;
see
[
38
]
.

Remark 12.16
.

Recall that on compact sets with the Markov property,
functions, that can be approximated by polynomials in an
[M]
-rapid manner,
admit extensions to functions of Denjoy–Carleman type on the ambient space by a continuous linear map.
But this extension involves a loss of regularity; cf.
Section

9.5
.
In the framework of Braun–Meise–Taylor classes there is a class of weight functions, namely strong weight functions
\omega
such that
15
15

15

Note that (
12.10
) implies that
\omega
is strong, e.g. by
Lemma

12.2
.

(12.10)

\;\exists L>1\;\forall t\geq 0:\omega(t^{2})\leq L\omega(t)+L,

for which the analogous problem allows a solution without loss of regularity (at least in the Beurling case), see

[
39
]
. For instance,
the weight functions
\omega_{s}(t)=\max\{0,(\log t)^{s}\}
,
s>1
, satisfy (
12.10
).

13.
Extensions not preserving the ultradifferentiable class

As for Denjoy–Carleman classes it is natural to ask
whether the loss of regularity in the extension, for weight functions that are not strong,
can be determined and controlled.

13.1.
The singleton

For the singleton we have

Theorem 13.1

(
[
18
]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a non-quasianalytic weight function, and
\sigma
another weight function.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\exists C>0\;\forall t>0:\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(tu)}{u^{2}}\,du\leq C\sigma(t)+C
.

(2)

\mathcal{E}^{\{\sigma\}}(\{0\})\subseteq j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(3)

\mathcal{E}^{(\sigma)}(\{0\})\subseteq j^{\infty}_{\{0\}}\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

This result was extended to compact convex sets with non-empty interior, by Langenbruch
[
54
]
.
The proofs are based on descriptions of the duals of the spaces at hand as weighted spaces of
entire functions.

13.2.
Roumieu extension on arbitrary closed sets

The general case was recently solved in the Roumieu case:

Theorem 13.2

(
[
78
,
79
]
)
.

Let
\omega
be a non-quasianalytic concave weight function and
\sigma
a weight function with
\sigma(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\exists C>0\;\forall t>0:\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(tu)}{u^{2}}\,du\leq C\sigma(t)+C
.

(2)

\mathcal{E}^{\{\sigma\}}(A)\subseteq j^{\infty}_{A}\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for each closed subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Let us comment on the proof (of (1)
\Rightarrow
(2)).
For convenience we call a pair
(\sigma,\omega)
of weight functions as in the theorem and satisfying (1)

admissible
.
The two crucial ingredients are

•

cutoff functions with bounds that reflect the condition (1),

•

the extension method of Dyn’kin by Taylor approximation to higher and higher degree as
A
is approached.

Easy modifications in the proof of
Theorem

12.10
yield the desired cutoff functions:

Theorem 13.3

(
[
78
, Proposition 4.1]
)
.

Let
(\sigma,\omega)
be an admissible pair of weight functions.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
there exist
m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
,
C>0
and
0<r_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
such that
for all
0<r<r_{0}
there exist
\varphi_{n,r}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with the following properties:

(1)

0\leq\varphi_{n,r}\leq 1
.

(2)

\varphi_{n,r}|_{[-r,r]}=1
and
\operatorname{supp}\varphi_{n,r}\subseteq[-\frac{9}{8}r,\frac{9}{8}r]
.

(3)

We have

(13.1)

\|\varphi_{n,r}\|^{\omega}_{\mathbb{R},m}\leq C\exp\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sigma^{\star}(nr)\Big).

Here
\sigma^{\star}
is the conjugate of
\sigma
defined in (
12.7
).

Note that
\kappa(t):=\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(ut)}{u^{2}}\,du
,
t>0
, is a (possibly quasianalytic) weight function
and
(\kappa,\omega)
is an admissible pair.
If
(\sigma,\omega)
is another admissible pair, then
\kappa(t)=O(\sigma(t))
as
t\to\infty
, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}^{[\sigma]}\subseteq\mathcal{E}^{[\kappa]}
.
Moreover,
\kappa
is concave; cf.
Lemma

12.2
. It follows that we may assume without loss of generality that
\sigma
is concave.

In order to implement Dyn’kin’s extension procedure one
uses the description of Braun–Meise–Taylor spaces of functions and jets by the associated weight matrix, see

Theorem

11.4
.
In view of
Remark

11.19
we may assume that there is a weight matrix
\mathfrak{S}=\{S^{x}\}_{x>0}

such that
\mathcal{E}^{[\sigma]}=\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{S}]}
,
where each
S^{x}
is strongly log-convex and

\sup_{j,k}\Big(\frac{s^{x}_{j+k}}{s^{2x}_{j}s^{2x}_{k}}\Big)=H<\infty

which entails

(13.2)

h_{s^{x}}(t)\leq h_{s^{2x}}(Ht)^{2},\quad t>0,~x>0.

Setting
t^{x}_{k}:=\min_{0\leq j\leq k}s^{2x}_{j}s^{2x}_{k-j}
we obtain another
weight matrix
\mathfrak{T}=\{T^{x}\}_{x>0}

such that
\mathcal{E}^{[\sigma]}=\mathcal{E}^{[\mathfrak{T}]}
,
where each
T^{x}
is strongly log-convex and

\Big(\frac{t^{x}_{k}}{t^{x}_{k-1}}\Big)_{k\geq 1}=\Big(\frac{s^{2x}_{1}}{s^{2x}_{0}},\frac{s^{2x}_{1}}{s^{2x}_{0}},\frac{s^{2x}_{2}}{s^{2x}_{1}},\frac{s^{2x}_{2}}{s^{2x}_{1}},\frac{s^{2x}_{3}}{s^{2x}_{2}},\frac{s^{2x}_{3}}{s^{2x}_{2}},\ldots\Big)

which implies (cf. (
7.4
))

(13.3)

2\Gamma_{s^{2x}}=\Gamma_{t^{x}}.

Now, if
F=(F^{\alpha})_{\alpha}
is a Whitney ultrajet of class
\mathcal{E}^{\{\sigma\}}
on some compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
then there exists
x>0
such that
F\in\mathcal{E}^{\{T^{x}\}}(K)
.
Let
(Q_{j})_{j\geq 1}
be a family of Whitney cubes for
K
and
(\varphi_{j,\epsilon})_{j\geq 1}
a corresponding partition of
unity based on the cutoff functions from
Theorem

13.3
. Let
x_{j}
be the center of
Q_{j}
and
\hat{x}_{j}\in K

with
|\hat{x}_{j}-x_{j}|=d_{K}(x_{j})
.
We define

f(z):=\begin{cases}\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}(z)T^{p(x_{j})}_{\hat{x}_{j}}F(z)&\text{ if }z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus K,\\
F^{0}(z)&\text{ if }z\in K,\end{cases}

where

p(z):=2\Gamma_{s^{2x}}(Ld_{K}(z))-1

and
L
is a positive constant.
Then is not difficult to mimic the steps sketched in
Section

10.3

(in particular, choosing
\epsilon
and
L
suitably)
to see that
f
indeed provides an extension of class
\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}
.
The properties (
13.2
) and (
13.3
) serve as substitutes of (
7.9
) and (
7.10
).
The connection between the bound (
13.1
) and the functions
h_{s^{x}}
is established
by the observation (see
[
78
, Corollary 3.11]
)

\;\forall x>0\;\exists 0<c\leq 1\;\forall t>0:e^{c\sigma^{\star}(t)}\leq\frac{e}{h_{s^{x}}(ct)}.

For a detailed presentation we refer to
[
79
]
.

Remark 13.4
.

The proof shows that, if
\mathfrak{S}
and
\mathfrak{W}
are weight matrices with
\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{S}\}}=\mathcal{E}^{\{\sigma\}}
and

\mathcal{E}^{\{\mathfrak{W}\}}=\mathcal{E}^{\{\omega\}}
, respectively, then
for each
S\in\mathfrak{S}
and
a>0
there exist
W\in\mathfrak{W}
,
b>0
, and an extension operator

\mathcal{E}^{S}_{a}(K)\to\mathcal{E}^{W}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

13.3.
Beurling extension on arbitrary closed sets

We intend to reduce the Beurling to the Roumieu case (in a way similar to
Theorem

10.8
).
So, for an admissible pair of weight functions
(\sigma,\omega)

and for a Whitney ultrajet
F
of class
\mathcal{E}^{(\sigma)}
,
we would like to find
an admissible pair of weight functions
(\tilde{\sigma},\tilde{\omega})

such that
F
is also of Roumieu class
\mathcal{E}^{\{\tilde{\sigma}\}}
and
\omega(t)=o(\tilde{\omega}(t))
as
t\to\infty
.
Then we could infer from
Theorem

13.2
that
F
has an extension of class
\mathcal{E}^{\{\tilde{\omega}\}}

and hence of class
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}
, thanks to
\omega(t)=o(\tilde{\omega}(t))
as
t\to\infty
.

Unfortunately, it is not clear how to transfer the condition

\;\exists C>0\;\forall t>0:\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(tu)}{u^{2}}\,du\leq C\sigma(t)+C

to the pair
(\tilde{\sigma},\tilde{\omega})
(in such a way that also all the other requirements are fulfilled).
But a stronger condition can be transferred:
let
\omega
be a non-quasianalytic concave weight function and
\sigma
a weight function with
\sigma(t)=o(t)
as
t\to\infty
.
We say that the pair
(\sigma,\omega)
is
strongly admissible
if

(13.4)

\exists r\in(0,1)\;\exists C>0\;\forall t>0:\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{\omega(tu)}{u^{1+r}}\,du\leq C\sigma(t)+C.

It is obvious that a strongly admissible pair is admissible.
On the other hand
(\omega_{\alpha-1},\omega_{\alpha})
, where
\omega_{\alpha}(t)=t(\log t)^{-\alpha}
and
\alpha>1
,
is admissible, but not strongly admissible; see
[
73
, Example 11]
.

Lemma 13.5

(
[
73
, Lemma 13]
)
.

Let
(\sigma,\omega)
be a strongly admissible pair of weight functions
and
f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
any function satisfying
\sigma(t)=o(f(t))
as
t\to\infty
.
Then there exists a strongly admissible pair of weight functions
(\tilde{\sigma},\tilde{\omega})

such that

\text{$\omega(t)=o(\tilde{\omega}(t))$, $\sigma(t)=o(\tilde{\sigma}(t))$, and
$\tilde{\sigma}(t)=o(f(t))$ as $t\to\infty$}.

For the proof of the lemma it is crucial that
(
13.4
) is equivalent to

(13.5)

\exists C>0~\exists K>H>1~\exists t_{0}\geq 0~\forall t\geq t_{0}~\forall j\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}:\omega(K^{j}t)\leq CH^{j}\sigma(t);

see
[
73
, Proposition 7]
.
As a consequence (invoking also
Lemma

12.2
),
the pair
(\omega,\omega)
is strongly admissible if and only if
\omega
is strong; see
[
73
, Lemma 8]
.

Thanks to
Lemma

13.5
, the reduction scheme alluded to above then gives

Theorem 13.6

(
[
73
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
(\sigma,\omega)
be a strongly admissible pair of weight functions.
Then for every closed
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}

we have
\mathcal{E}^{(\sigma)}(A)\subseteq j^{\infty}_{A}(\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.

It is an open question if the conclusion holds for admissible pairs
(\sigma,\omega)
.

13.4.
Extension operators

As a by-product we obtain an extension operator on certain subspaces of
\mathcal{E}^{(\sigma)}(A)
with values in
\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Theorem 13.7

(
[
73
, Theorem 5]
)
.

Let
(\sigma,\omega)
be a strongly admissible pair of weight functions.
If
\tau
is a weight function with
\sigma(t)=o(\tau(t))
as
t\to\infty
, then for each non-empty closed subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}

there is an extension operator
\mathcal{E}^{\{\tau\}}(A)\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Applying
Lemma

13.5
to
f=\tau
, yields a strongly admissible pair
(\tilde{\sigma},\tilde{\omega})

satisfying

\omega(t)=o(\tilde{\omega}(t))
,
\sigma(t)=o(\tilde{\sigma}(t))
, and

\tilde{\sigma}(t)=o(\tau(t))
as
t\to\infty
.
Let
K
be a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then we have continuous inclusions
\mathcal{E}^{\{\tau\}}(K)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{(\tilde{\sigma})}(K)
and

\mathcal{E}^{\{\tilde{\omega}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Composing these maps with

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{(\tilde{\sigma})}(K)~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\tilde{S}}_{1}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\tilde{W}}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{n})~\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\{\tilde{\omega}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

where the middle arrow is the extension operator from
Remark

13.4
,
gives an extension operator
\mathcal{E}^{\{\tau\}}(K)\to\mathcal{E}^{(\omega)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Here
\tilde{S}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
,
\tilde{W}\in\tilde{\mathfrak{W}}
, and
\tilde{\mathfrak{S}}
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{W}}
are weight matrices associated with
\tilde{\sigma}
,
\tilde{\omega}
,
respectively.
If
A
is a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then we may use a suitable partition of unity
in order to construct the required extension operator.

Remark 13.8
.

All extensions in
Sections

12
and
13

can be chosen to be analytic in the complement of
A
.
This follows from a result of Schmets and Validivia
[
83
]
or
by adapting the proof of Langenbruch
[
56
, Theorem 13]
.
